(vii)



Government commercial concerns, accounts of which are subject to audit by
the Comptroller and Auditor General of India (CAG), fall under the following
categories:

(i) Government companies,
(ii) Statutory corporations and

(iii) | Departmentally managed commercial undertakings.

2. This Report deals with results of audit of Government companies and
Statutory corporations and has been prepared for submission to Government of
West Bengal under Section 19A of the CAG (Duties, Powers and Conditions
of Service) Act, 1971, as amended from time to time. Results of audit relating
to departmentally managed commercial undertakings are presented separately.

3. Audit of accounts of Government companies is conducted by the CAG
under provisions of Section 619 of the Companies Act, 1956.

4. In respect of West Bengal Industrial Infrastructure Development
Corporation, West Bengal Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes
Development and Finance Corporation, West Bengal Minorities Development
and Finance Corporation, West Bengal Backward Classes Development and
Finance Corporation and Calcutta, North and South Bengal State Transport
Corporations, which are Statutory corporations, the CAG is sole auditor. The
CAG also audits accounts of West Bengal Electricity Regulatory Commission,
as sole auditor. As per the State Financial Corporations (Amendment) Act
2000, the CAG has the right to conduct audit of accounts of West Bengal
Financial Corporation (WBFC) in addition to audit conducted by Chartered
Accountants appointed by WBFC out of a panel of auditors approved by the
Reserve Bank of India. In respect of West Bengal State Warehousing
Corporation, the CAG has the right to conduct audit of their accounts in
addition to audit conducted by the Chartered Accountants appointed by State
Government in consultation with the CAG. Audit Reports on annual accounts
of all these corporations/commission are forwarded separately to State
Government.

5. Cases mentioned in this Report are those, which came to notice in
course of audit during the year 2012-2013 as well as those which came to
notice in earlier years but were not dealt with in previous Reports. Matters
relating to period subsequent to 2012-2013 have also been included, wherever
necessary.

6. Audit has been conducted in accordance with Auditing Standards
issued by the Comptroller and Auditor General of India.

(ix)


Overview

1 Overview of State Public Sector Undertakings

Audit of Government companies is governed
by Section 619 of the Companies Act, 1956.
Accounts of Government companies are
audited by Statutory Auditors appointed by
the CAG. These accounts are also subject
to supplementary audit conducted by the
CAG. Audit of Statutory corporations is
governed by their respective legislations.
As on 31 March 2013, the State of West
Bengal had 74 working PSUs (65 companies
and nine Statutory corporations) and 17 non-
working PSUs (16 companies and one
corporation), which employed 0.65 lakh
employees. The 31 working PSUs that
finalised accounts for 2012-13 registered a
turnover of € 36,755.18 crore. This turnover
was equal to 6.37 per cent of State GDP.

Investments in PSUs

‘As on 31 March 2013, investment (capital
and long term loans) in 91 PSUs was
% 41,074.93 crore. It grew over & 34,754.79
crore in 2007-08, at a compound annual
growth rate of 3.4 per cent. Power sector
accounted for nearly 65.11 per cent of total
investment in 2012-13. Government
contributed € 968.91 crore towards equity,
loans and grants/ subsidies during 2012-13.

Performance of PSUs

Out of 74 working PSUs, 31 prepared
accounts for 2012-13. During the year
2012-13, 19 PSUs earned profit of f 688.28
crore and 11 PSUs incurred loss of
= 1,231.24 crore. Major contributors to profit
were West Bengal State Electricity
Transmission Company Limited

(€ 333.02 crore), The West Bengal Power
Development Corporation Limited
(% 131.66 crore), West Bengal State
Electricity Distribution Company Limited
(© 81.72 crore) and West Bengal Housing
Infrastructure Development Corporation
Limited % 62.36 crore). Heavy losses were
incurred by Haldia Petrochemicals Limited
(© 960.11 crore), The Calcutta Tramways
Company (1978) Limited (₹ 137.02 crore)
and The Durgapur Projects Limited
(₹ 68.03 crore).

Arrears in accounts and quality of
accounts

Quality of accounts of PSUs needs
improvement. Reports of Statutory Auditors
on internal control of companies indicated
several weak areas.

The accounts of 43 working PSUs were in
arrears for periods ranging from one to four
years, There were 17 non-working PSUs of
which three finalised their accounts for the
year 2012-13 while 14 PSUs had arrears of
accounts for one to seven years. State
Government needs to take appropriate action
to clear arrears in a time bound manner.

Placement of SARs

There was delay in placement of SARs in
State Legislature in respect of Statutory
Corporations. The Government should
ensure prompt placement of SARs in the
Legislature.

(Chapter 1)

2 Performance audits relating to Government Company

Performance Audit relating to ‘Implementation of Rajiv Gandhi Grameen
Vidyutikaran Yojana (RGGVY)’ in respect of West Bengal State Electricity
Distribution Company Limited was conducted. Executive summary of audit
findings is given next:

(xi)


Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended March 2013

Introduction

Rajiv Gandhi Grameen Vidyutikaran Yojana
(RGGVY) was launched (April 2005) by
Government of India (Gol) as a flagship
programme to provide access to electricity
to all rural households by 2009. As on
March 2013, 12,594 villages with 9.92 lakh
BPL rural households (RHHs) were
electrified by West Bengal State Electricity
Distribution Company Limited (WBSEDCL)
under RGGVY scheme against the target of
14,113 villages with 15.39 lakh BPL RHHs
by 2009. Out of 997.98 crore provided
by Government of India, for electrification
of nine districts and one Mahakuma Parishad
(Siliguri), WBSEDCL spent & 923.63 crore
till March 2013.

Planning and Preparation of Detailed
Project Reports (DPRs)

WBSEDCL prepared DPRs without
adequate field survey. One hundred fifty
six villages that did not qualify under
RGGVY were included in DPRs and
£11.49 crore was estimated for their
electrification.

Inclusion of sales tax and sundries which
should not be included in estimates increased
the project cost by % 37.79 crore for
10 projects.

Contract Management

Estimates put to tender were inflated by
₹ 62.36 crore due to inclusion of sales tax,
sundries, franchisee charges and overhead
charges which were not payable to the
contractors. WBSEDCL allowed different
rates for ex-works price of same material to
same contractors working on different
packages in the same or adjoining districts.
This resulted in extra expenditure of
₹ 18.92 crore.

WBSEDCL released & 145.43 crore as
interest free mobilisation advance to
contractors. Due to delay in completion of
works 219.63 crore remained unadjusted
which led to loss of interest of 1.51 crore.

Sales tax of हैं 8.32 crore was paid to
contractors in- spite of exemption of sales
tax on materials used in RGGVY. Similarly,
excise duty of 97.40 lakh and service tax
of & 74.79 lakh were paid to contractors in
excess of prevailing rates of taxation.
Project execution

Despite delays in execution of works,
liquidated damages of & 57.86 crore were
not levied as per terms of Letters of Award
for work.

In 217 villages of six districts, service
connections could not be provided to 15,433
BPL RHHs inspite of creating infrastructure
at a cost of € 6.25 crore.

Scheme impact

WBSEDCL energised 2,412 villages after
delays of 16 to 829 days from creation of
required infrastructure.

Delays in billing beyond 90 days resulted
in accumulation of dues. As a result
14,513 consumers with aggregate dues of
% 1.95 crore had defaulted in payment and
consequently supply was disconnected
defeating objective of the scheme.

Financial operation

Due to delayed opening of a separate
RGGVY bank account by nine to 25 days,
interest of € 0.39 crore could not be earned
and credited to RGGVY account. Further,
₹ 1.99 crore as interest could not be earned
as RGGVY fund of € 5.86 crore was retained
(2009-13) in corporate account of
WBSEDCL.

Project monitoring

Third party inspection agencies (TPIAs)
inspected 4,939 villages and reported
42,677 defects against which only
12,679 defects were rectified till March
2013. At SMP, Birbhum and Nadia
96, 41 and 30 villages out of 127, 91 and
124 villages respectively were inspected by
TPIAs after completion of guarantee period
of one year, defeating the purpose of
inspection.

Conclusion and recommendations

DPRs were prepared without drawing up
RE plan or conducting field surveys leading
to omission of BPL RHHs and inclusion of
clectrified/ unsuitable villages. Tenders
were floated without due diligence leading
to variations in prices of the same materials
and excess payment of taxes and duties.
Work had been delayed on account of belated
delivery of materials, inadequate deployment
of manpower, lack of co-ordination etc.
leading to shortfall in the numbers of BPL
RHHS electrified. Progress of work was not
commensurate with the quantum of fund
received. Moreover, monitoring was
ineffective.

There are four recommendations - DPRs
need to be prepared with proper field surveys,
appropriate clauses be incorporated in
contracts to safeguard the interest of
WBSEDCL, applicability of correct rate of
taxes and duties be ensured and system of
monitoring needs to be strengthened.

(Chapter 2)

(xii)


Overview

3 Performance audits relating to Statutory Corporation

Another Performance Audit relating to ‘Financial Assistance to entrepreneurs
and recovery of loans’ of West Bengal Financial Corporation was conducted.
Executive summary of audit findings is given below:

Introduction

‘West Bengal Financial Corporation (WBFC)
was set up in 1954 under the State Financial
Corporations Act, 1951. It is the key state
level lending institution for the Micro, Small
and Medium Enterprises (MiSMB), with a
Head Office at Kolkata, Regional Offices at
Siliguri, Durgapur and Kolkata and 11 Branch
Offices throughout the State.

WBFC is managed by a Board of Directors
consisting of 10 Directors including
Managing Director and four directors
nominated by financial institutions. The
Managing Director of WBFC is the chief
executive.

Operational and financial performance

Despite being the key state level lending
institution for financing MiSME, WBFC
financed only 2.10 per cent of new units that
had come up in last five years. WBFC
became increasingly dependent on bonds as
SIDBI, being its source of finance, had
gradually phased out re-finance loans.
WBFC’s equity base of % 147.35 crore was
almost wiped out due to accumulated losses
of& 128.42 crore. Its non-performing assets
remained above 26 per cent at the end of
2012-13. It had to bear avoidable interest
burden due to absence of proper planning
for redemption of bonds.

Sanction and disbursement of loan

WBEC had framed (February 2008) an
entrepreneur evaluation policy which seemed
to discourage sanction of loan to first time
entrepreneurs. WBFC also did not subscribe
to the Credit Guarantee Fund Trust Scheme
for Micro and Small Enterprises (MiSE).
This was another obstacle for new MiSEs to
receive loans.

WBFC did not prepare industry-wise
appraisal manual. It did not rate credit risk
or subscribe to Credit Information Bureau
(India) Limited for credit history of borrowers
until January 2013.

Instances of deficient appraisal, absence of
due diligence, incorrect assessment of
working capital and acceptance of flawed
security in appraisal, sanction and
disbursement of loans were noticed in audit.

Audit also observed high concentration of
credit to defaulting sectors and loans to
ineligible borrowers.

Monitoring and recovery performance

WBFC had adopted monitoring and follow
up guideline in January 2008. However, it
did not ask for progress report of the projects
or the annual accounts from the borrowers.
Low number of inspections coupled with
failure to verify books of accounts of
borrowers resulted in default in repayment
of loans.

WBFC failed to invoke the provisions of the
State Financial Corporations Act, 1951 as
also adopt the procedures specified in the
recovery manual to promptly recover its dues
from defaulting borrowers.

Internal Control and Internal Audit

Allocation of duties and responsibilities was
diffused. Besides, the requirement of having
updated information on borrowers as per
‘Know your client’ prescribed by Reserve
Bank was not followed.

Conclusion and Recommendations

WBEC was not a significant source of
financing for MiSME units in the State. Its
appraisal mechanism was inadequate. WBFC
did not follow its own monitoring and follow-
up guideline. Internal control processes were
weak.

There are three recommendations —
Government may review the objectives and
viability of WBFC. Further WBFC should
strengthen its appraisal mechanism and ensure
greater compliance with its monitoring and
follow-up guidelines.

(Chapter 3)

(xiii)


Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended March 2013

4 Compliance audit

Compliance audit observations included in this Report highlight deficiencies in
the management of PSUs, which resulted in serious financial implications. The
irregularities pointed out are broadly of the following nature:

. Loss of = 190.10 crore due to undue favour/ benefit to contractors in two
cases.

(Paragraphs 4.1 and 4.5)

. Loss of % 42.85 crore due to non-safeguarding of financial interests of
organisation in two cases.

(Paragraphs 4.7 and 4.9)
. Defective/ deficient planning in one case involving € 19.58 crore.
(Paragraph 4.2)

. Non-compliance with statutes/ rules/ directives/ procedures in three cases
involving हैं 17.20 crore.

(Paragraphs 4.3, 4.8 and 4.10)
. Inadequate/ deficient monitoring in two cases involving ¥ 2.78 crore.

(Paragraphs 4.4 and 4.6)
Gist of some of the important audit observations is given below:

The West Bengal Power Development Corporation Limited (WBPDCL) and The Durgapur
Projects Limited selected a private-sector partner, to set up a joint venture (JV) company for
operation of captive coal mines, without open tender. Moreover, there were contractual drawbacks
in terms and conditions. Further, WBPDCL pursued with Ministry of Coal for allotment of coking
coal block without ascertaining the suitability of the coal available therein and incurred avoidable
expenditure of & 6.68 crore.

(Paragraphs 4.1 and 4.2)

West Bengal State Electricity Distribution Company Limited (WBSEDCL) incurred additional
interest of € 1.83 crore on avoidable cash credit availed arising from lack of monitoring of daily
remittances of total collections by banker. Further, WBSEDCL determined service charges for
collection of bills at a higher rate on account of calculation on incorrect data leading to excess
expenditure of 1.25 crore.

(Paragraphs 4.4 and 4.5)

Mackintosh Burn Limited and West Bengal Industrial Infrastructure Development Corporation
persistently failed to bill service tax on recipients of their services and had to incur avoidable
expenditure of € 7.98 crore towards service tax and interest thereon.

(Paragraph 4.8)

Calcutta State Transport Corporation paid excess incentive of € 1.44 crore to its crew members
on long distance routes.

(Paragraph 4.9)

West Bengal State Warchousing Corporation suffered loss of warehousing charges of € 3.30
crore due to inadequacies in recovery system.

(Paragraph 4.10)

1 Overview of State Public Sector Undertakings

11 Introduction

1.1.1 The State Government’s Public Sector Undertakings (PSUs) comprise
81 Government Companies and 10 Statutory Corporations. As on 31 March
2013, there were 91 PSUs as per the details given in table 1.1. Of these, only
one Company! was listed on the stock exchange(s). They employed a total of
64,996 employees as of 31 March 20137.

1.1.2 Out of total 91 PSUs, among the working PSUs, 31 State PSUs® had
finalised their annual accounts for 2012-13 as of September 2013, registering a
turnover of % 36,755.18 crore, which was equal to 6.37 per cent of State Gross
Domestic Product (0797) for 2012-13.’ Besides the State PSUs, there were
also 10 Departmental Undertakings (DUs) which carried out commercial
operations. Audit findings in respect of these DUs are incorporated in other
Audit Reports for the State under the respective departments.

1.1.3. During the year 2012-13, audit of West Bengal Highway Development
Corporation Limited and Kolkata Police Housing Infrastructure Development
Corporation Limited were added to audit jurisdiction.

1.2... Audit Mandate

1.2.1 Audit of Government companies is governed by Section 619 of the
Companies Act, 1956. According to Section 617, a Government company is
one in which not less than 51 per cent of the paid up capital is held by the
Government(s). A Government company includes a subsidiary of a
Government company. Further, a Company in which 51 per cent of the paid
up capital is held in any combination by the Government(s), Government
companies and Corporations controlled by Government(s) is treated as a
Deemed Government Company as per Section 619-B of the Companies Act.

' WEBFIL Limited.

working) did not furnish the details till December 2013.

3 Non-working PSUs are those which have ceased to carry on their operations.

* Includes three 619-B companies.

> Thirty Government companies and one Statutory corporation.

® Advance Estimates at Current Prices- € 5,76,865 crore.

7 The working results of all 91 PSUs in the State based on their latest finalised accounts show
a total turnover of € 39,118.49 crore (6.78 per cent of GSDP) and net loss of € 882.45 crore.



Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

1.2.2. The accounts of the Government companies (as defined in Section 617
of the Companies Act, 1956) are audited by Statutory Auditors, who are
appointed by the Comptroller and Auditor General of India (CAG) as per the
provisions of Section 619(2) of the Companies Act, 1956. These accounts are
also subject to supplementary audit conducted by the CAG as per the
provisions of Section 619 of the Companies Act, 1956.

1.2.3. Audit of Statutory corporations is governed by their respective
legislations. Out of nine working Statutory corporations, the CAG is the sole
auditor for seven’. In respect of West Bengal State Warehousing Corporation
and West Bengal Financial Corporation, the audit is conducted by Chartered
Accountants and supplementary audit is done by the CAG. In case of one
non-working corporation, i.e, Great Eastern Hotel Authority, only
Compliance audit is undertaken by the CAG.

liquidation process in respect of the remaining eight had not started. The
process of voluntary winding up under the Companies Act is much faster and
needs to be adopted/ pursued vigorously. Delays in winding up of the
remaining closed companies were attributed (September 2012) by the
Government to the Government’s nominee directors’ unwillingness to furnish
the requisite indemnity bond required for winding up of these companies. The
only closed corporation could not be wound up as all the accounts related to it
were not yet closed. The Government may take a decision regarding revival/
winding up of non-working PSUs.

1.6.4 The State Government invested 271,443.80 crore (Equity:
% 60.95 crore, Loans: ¥ 194.84 crore and Grants/ Subsidy: & 1,188.01 crore) in
42 working PSUs during the years from 2008-09 to 2012-13 for which
accounts have not been finalised as detailed in Annexure 1.5. In the absence
of accounts and their subsequent audit, it cannot be ensured whether
investments made and expenditure incurred has been properly accounted for
and the purposes of investments have been achieved or not. Hence
Government’s investment in such PSUs remains outside the scrutiny of the
State Legislature. Moreover, delay in finalisation of accounts also bears the
risk of fraud and leakage of public money apart from violation of the
provisions of the Companies Act, 1956.

1.6.5 Administrative departments have the responsibility to oversee the
activities of these entities and to ensure that the accounts are finalised and
adopted by these PSUs within the prescribed period. The concerned
administrative departments and officials of the Government were informed
every quarter by the Principal Accountant General (PAG)/ Accountant
General (AG), of the arrears in finalisation of accounts, but no remedial
measure was taken. As a result of this, the net worth of these PSUs could not
be assessed in audit. Arrears of accounts were addressed to (April, July and
December 2013) the Chief Secretary/ Finance Secretary by the AG to expedite
clearance of backlog in accounts in a time bound manner.

1.6.6 In view of above state of arrears, it is recommended that the
Government may monitor and ensure timely finalisation of accounts of the
PSUs with special focus on liquidation of arrears and compliance with the
provisions of the Companies Act, 1956.

1.11.1 Outstanding departmental replies on paragraphs appeared in the
Audit Reports

Reports of the CAG encompass observations arising out of scrutiny of
accounts and transactions of various Government companies and Statutory
corporations. Therefore, it is necessary that the executives give appropriate
and timely responses to them. Finance Department, Government of West
Bengal instructed (October 2009) all the administrative departments to submit

11


Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

illustrative notes to the West Bengal Legislative Assembly with corrective/
remedial action taken or proposed to be taken on the observations included in
the Audit Reports within two months from the date of presentation of laying of
the Audit Reports in the State Legislature.

Though the Audit Reports for the years 2003-04, 2004-05, 2005-06, 2006-
07, 2007-08, 2008-09, 2009-10 and 2010-11 were presented to the State
Legislature in August 2005, July 2006, March 2007, March 2008,
July 2009, July 2010, September 2011 and September 2012 respectively,
14 departments, whose activities were commented upon did not submit
their explanatory notes on 30 out of 196 paragraphs/ Performance audits as
of September 2013 (Annexure 1.8).

1.11.2 Outstanding action taken notes on the Reports of the Committee on
Public Undertakings (COPU)

Reports of the COPU presented to the Legislature contain recommendations
and observations on which administrative departments are required to submit
their Action Taken Notes (ATNs) within six weeks from the date of receipt of
COPU recommendations. Even after lapse of one month to 62 months,
10 departments did not furnish the ATNs on 70 recommendations relating to
26COPU Reports presented (July 2008 - August 2013) to the State
Legislature (Annexure 1.9).

1.11.3 Response to the Inspection reports, draft paragraphs and
Performance audits

Anomalies/ deficiencies noticed during the periodical inspections of
Government companies as well as Statutory corporations and not settled on
the spot are communicated through the Inspection Reports (IRs) to the
respective heads of PSUs and the concerned departments of the State
Government. The heads of PSUs are required to furnish their replies to the
IRs through the respective heads of the departments within a period of four
weeks. A half yearly report is being sent to the Principal Secretary/ Secretary
of the departments in respect of pending IRs to facilitate monitoring of the
audit observations in those IRs.

Inspection Reports issued up to September 2013 pertaining to 44 PSUs
disclosed that 148 paragraphs relating to 55 IRs remained outstanding at the
end of September 2013. The department-wise break up of IRs and audit
observations as of September 2013 is given in Annexure 1.10. In order to
expedite settlement of the outstanding paragraphs, Audit Committees were
constituted in 16 out of 23 departments. These Committees were to meet, at
least, once every month. However, during October 2012 to September 2013,
two audit committee meetings were held by the Power and Non-Conventional
Energy Sources department.

Similarly, draft paragraphs and Performance audits on the working of PSUs
are forwarded to the Principal Secretary/ Secretary of the administrative
department concerned demi-officially, seeking confirmation of the facts and
figures and their comments thereon within a period of six weeks. It was,

12


Chapter I Overview of State Public Sector Undertakings

however, noticed that two draft paragraphs forwarded to two departments
during July to August 2013, as detailed in Annexure 1.11 had not been replied
so far (December 2013).

It is recommended that the Government should ensure that (a) procedure
exists for action against the officials who fail to submit replies to inspection
reports/ draft paragraphs/ Performance audit reports and ATNs on
recommendations of COPU, as per the prescribed time schedule; (b) action to
recover loss/ outstanding advances/ overpayment is taken within the
prescribed period and (c) system of responding to audit observations is
revamped.



Chapter II

West Bengal State Electricity Distribution Company Limited

Implementation of Rajiv Gandhi Grameen Vidyutikaran Yojana (RGGVY)

Executive Summary

Introduction

Rajiv Gandhi Grameen  Vidyutikaran
Yojana (RGGVY) was launched (April 2005)
by Government of India (Gol) as a flagship
programme to provide access to electricity to
all rural households by 2009. As on March
2013, 12,594 villages with 9.92 lakh BPL
rural households (RHHs) were electrified by
West Bengal State Electricity Distribution
Company Limited (WBSEDCL) under
RGGVY scheme against the target of
14,113 villages with 15.39 lakh BPL RHHs
by 2009. Out of ₹997.98 crore provided by
Government of India, for electrification of
nine districts and one Mahakuma Parishad
(Siliguri),  WBSEDCL had spent
923.63 crore till March 2013.

Planning and Preparation of Detailed
Project Reports (DPRs)

WBSEDCL prepared DPRs__ without
adequate field survey. One hundred fifty six
villages that did not qualify under RGGVY
were included in DPRs and ®11.49 crore
was estimated for their electrification.

Inclusion of sales tax and sundries which
should not be included in estimates
increased the project cost by €37.79 crore
for 10 projects.

Contract Management

Estimates put to tender were inflated by
% 62.36 crore due to inclusion of sales tax,
sundries, franchisee charges and overhead
charges which were not payable to the
contractors. WBSEDCL allowed different
rates for ex-works price of same material to
same contractors working on di
packages in the same or adjoining districts.
This resulted in extra expenditure of
18.92 crore,

WBSEDCL released 145.43 crore as
interest free mobilisation advance to
contractors. Due to delay in completion of
works 19.63 crore remained unadjusted
which led to loss of interest of €1.51 crore.

Sales tax of ®832 crore was paid to
contractors inspite of exemption of sales tax
on materials used in RGGVY. Similarly,
excise duty of €97.40 lakh and service tax of
274.79 lakh were paid to contractors in
excess of prevailing rates of taxation.

Project execution

Despite delays in execution of works,
liquidated damages of %57.86 crore were
not levied as per terms of Letters of Award
for work.

In 217 villages of six districts, service
connections could not be provided to
15,433 BPL RHHs inspite of creating
infrastructure at a cost of €6.25 crore.

Scheme impact

WBSEDCL energised 2,412 villages after
delays of 16 to 829 days from creation of
required infrastructure.

Delays in billing beyond 90 days resulted in
accumulation of dues. As a_ result
14,513 consumers with aggregate dues of
71.95 crore had defaulted in payment and
consequently supply was disconnected
defeating objective of the scheme.

Financial operation

Due to delayed opening of a separate
RGGVY bank account by nine to 25 days,
interest of €0.39 crore could not be earned
and credited to RGGVY account. Further,
71.99 crore as interest could not be earned
as RGGVY fund of &5.86 crore was retained

15


Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

(2009-13) in corporate account of
WBSEDCL.
Project monitoring

Third party inspection agencies (TPIAs)
inspected 4,939 villages and _ reported
42,677 defects against which —_ only
12,679 defects were rectified till March
2013. At SMP, Birbhum and Nadia 96, 41
and 30villages out of 127, 91 and
124 villages respectively were inspected by
TPIAs after completion of guarantee period
of one year, defeating the purpose of
inspection.

electrified/ unsuitable villages. Tenders were
floated without due diligence leading to
variations in prices of the same materials
and excess payment of taxes and duties.
Work had been delayed on account of
belated delivery of materials, inadequate
deployment of manpower, lack of co-
ordination, etc. leading to shortfall in the
numbers of BPL RHHs electrified. The
progress of work was not commensurate
with the quantum of fund received.
Moreover, monitoring was ineffective.

There are four recommendations - DPRs
need to be prepared with proper field

surveys, appropriate clauses be incorporated
in contracts to safeguard the interest of
WBSEDCL, applicability of correct rate of
taxes and duties be ensured and system of
monitoring needs to be strengthened.

Conclusion and recommendations

DPRs were prepared without drawing up RE
plan or conducting field surveys leading to
omission of BPL HHs and inclusion of

21 Introduction

Rajiv Gandhi — Grameen Vidyutikaran Yojana (RGGVY) was
launched (April 2005) by the Government of India (Gol) as a flagship
programme to provide access to electricity to all rural households (RHHs) by
2009 as envisaged in the Rural Electrification Policy (REPOL, August 2006).
It provided for creation of Rural Electricity Distribution Backbone (REDB’),
village electrification infrastructure and rural household electrification of
below poverty line (BPL) households free of cost. It also contained provisions
for appointment of franchisees for distribution of electricity in the rural areas
and ensuring revenue sustainability.

RGGVY was to be implemented through the Rural Electrification Corporation
Limited (REC), a Central PSU. Gol was to provide 90 per cent capital subsidy
to the State Government to meet overall cost of the projects, while the rest
10 per cent was to be borne by the implementing State.

The erstwhile WBSEB’ was envisaged as project implementing agency of
RGGVY in West Bengal and was entrusted (June 2005) with the responsibility
for formulation, development and implementation of the projects in ten’
districts of the state. The projects involved system planning, design and
engineering in line with REC’s guidelines, specifications and construction

standards, wherever applicable, and procurement in accordance with agreed
competitive bidding procedures. WBSEB was also required to construct/
implement/ commission the sub-transmission and distribution works under the
projects on behalf of the Government of West Bengal (GoWB).

In April 2007, WBSEB’s hydro-electric generation and distribution activities
were transferred to a new Government company viz. West Bengal State
Electricity Distribution Company Limited (WBSEDCL), consequent to the
restructuring of the power sector. Thereafter, WBSEDCL took over
implementation of RGGVY*.

2.2 Organisation structure

The Executive Director (Rural Electrification) of WBSEDCL, who reports to
the Chairman and Managing Director (CMD), is in overall charge of Rural
Electrification (RE) including implementing RGGVY. Day-to-day
management of the wing and execution are looked after by the Chief Engineer
(RE), assisted by 19 Project Managers in different districts. Besides, a State-
Level co-ordination committee was formed (December 2008) to co-ordinate
RGGVY works while District Rural Energy Committees were constituted
(May 2009) to supervise Rural Electrification (RE) works at the district and
block levels. Similarly, the Distribution wing under the Director
(Distribution), is managed by the Chief Engineer (Distribution) through five
Zonal Offices, 17 Regional Offices, 60 Divisions and 476 Customer Care
Centres as at March 2013. On completion of the RGGVY works and
energisation of BPL Rural Households (RHHs), the infrastructure is
transferred to the Distribution wing.

2.3 Audit objectives

The Performance Audit was undertaken to assess whether:
e Planning was in line with the RGGVY guidelines;
e Contracts were managed efficiently and economically;
e the projects were effectively implemented to fulfil the objectives of
RGGVY;
e 116 funds were utilised prudently and economically; and

e effective internal control system for monitoring the award of contracts,
execution of projects, and ensuring timely supply of quality power to
RHHs was in place.

2.4 Scope and methodology of audit
The Performance Audit was undertaken between April and August 2013. It

covered the activities of WBSEDCL with regard to RGGVY works executed
during the period from 2008-09 to 2012-13. The records at the head office of

* excluding Hill areas of Darjeeling district.



Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

WBSEDCL and eight* out of the total 10 project offices of WBSEDCL in
corresponding districts were examined by audit®,

Audit methodology included scrutiny of Detailed Project Reports (DPR),
project files and MIS reports. It also included beneficiary surveys of
519’ BPL RHH. Audit also examined correspondences and directives from
the Government and REC and minutes/ agenda papers of the Board meetings/
Review meetings of WBSEDCL. Further, Audit has cross checked data with
the master lists of BPL Rural Households prepared by the Department of
Panchayat and Rural Development, GoWB, whenever required.

Audit explained its objectives, criteria and methodology to WBSEDCL and
the State Government during an ‘Entry Conference’ held on 20 March 2013,
attended by the Principal Secretary to the Government of West Bengal, Power
and Non-Conventional Energy Sources Department, the Chairman and
Managing Director, WBSEDCL and senior management. The findings were
discussed at an ‘Exit Conference’ held on 10 December 2013.

2.5... Audit criteria

The main sources of audit criteria for the performance audit were:
Rural Electrification Policy 2006.
Scheme guidelines issued by the Ministry of Power, the Gol and
additional guidelines issued by REC regarding Quality control and
Procurement of goods and services etc.
Tripartite agreement among REC, GoWB and WBSEB of June 2005.
Instructions/ circulars/ orders issued by the Ministry of Power, the Gol
and REC regarding the scheme.

2.6 Audit Findings

Under X Plan (2002-07) RGGVY was launched in West Bengal (2005) and
was extended under XI Plan (2007-12) for intensification in those villages of
ten districts of West Bengal where the population exceeds 100. The
implementing agency was WBSEDCL. Apart from that, RGGVY was to be
implemented in four districts by Central PSUs. As on March 2013, 12,594
villages with 9.92 lakh BPL RHHs were electrified by WBSEDCL under
RGGVY scheme against the target of 14,113 villages covering 15.39 lakh
RHHs by 2009. The remaining 1,519 villages and 5.47 lakh RHHs were yet
to be electrified.

2.7 Planning and preparation of Detailed Project Reports (DPRs)

Planning involves identification of beneficiaries, choices of technology,
arrangements for management, preparation of cost estimates, setting of time
frame and success criteria and mechanism for ensuring revenue sustainability
of rural electricity supply.

2.7.1 Rural Electrification Plan

According to REPOL with its basic aim of providing access to electricity to
all rural households (RHHs) by 2009, GoWB” had to prepare a Rural
Electrification Plan (REP) in consultation with Gol by February 2007. The
REP would map and detail the delivery mechanism in respect of
electrification, considering the available technologies based on the available
sources of energy. It would identify the number of non-electrified RHHs and
their distance from the existing grid'°and estimate the required cost of rural
electrification (RE). The REPs would in turn be linked with District
Development Plans as and when such plan became available.

consultation with the Gol in September 2008 after a delay of 18 months. The
DPRs were already under preparation on the basis of inputs from the Zilla
Parishads without any reference of REP and those were finalised by
WBSEDCL between December 2005 and January 2008 and approved by REC
in April 2008. REP should provide inputs for the preparation of DPRs, but
due to the delay in preparation of REP, this was not possible. Audit observed
that there were deviations between the numbers of BPL RHHs included in the
REP and the DPRs and in three out of the ten districts the deviation was
significant (reference Table 2.2) which resulted in 1.91 lakh BPL RHHs
remaining outside RGGVY coverage due to their exclusion from the DPRs.

mentioned in the approved DPRs and audit scrutiny revealed that during 2008-

utilised by WBSEDCL. Therefore, paucity of fund was unlikely to be the
ground for reduction in the number of BPL RHHs in the DPR.

2.7.2 Detailed Project Reports

Preparation of DPRs for RGGVY required intensive field survey to identify
the BPL RHHs who would have access to electricity to the extent that their
homes would be provided with an energy meter, requisite wiring within the
RHHs and installation of one CFL'® with required fittings. This in turn
entailed that the necessary source of energy be identified from the distribution

72 Backward Region Grant Fund, a centrally sponsored scheme.
13 Compact Fluorescent Light bulb, provision for which was introduced subsequently in 2008.

Chapter II Performance audit relating to Government Company

network" within that village. Further, if the required distribution network was
not available, the same was to be created under RGGVY. The distribution
infrastructure thus created would also benefit the above poverty line (APL)
RHHs as the infrastructure would also be equally available to them for
obtaining electricity connections.

The WBSEDCL prepared the DPRs without adequate field survey. Audit
observed'® that in the absence of any field survey, the DPR included
156 villages not falling under RGGVY scheme, e.g., 55 villages where there
was no scope for further electrification as existing infrastructure was already
available and capable of catering to the existing demand. It also included
16 un-inhabited (non-abadi) villages and 20 villages which were non-existent.
Further, it included 16 villages which were under municipal jurisdiction (i.e.
urban areas) and therefore beyond the ambit of RGGVY. Besides, 49 villages
within the command areas of Eastern Coalfields Limited (ECL), CESC
Limited (CESC) and Sriniketan and Santiniketan Development Authority area
which were not to be included were found included, as a result of inadequate
field survey.

Inclusion of 156 non-eligible villages in the DPRs where RGGVY works
could not be executed led to overestimation of project cost by € 11.49 crore'®.
Moreover, those 26 villages within CESC’s command area would continue to
remain outside the ambit of any RE scheme.

Management accepted (November 2013) the audit observation and attributed
the mistake to preparation of field surveys by outside agencies who in turn
depended on the assistance of the local representatives and also on the absence
of demarcation between villages and habitations as well as command zones of
ECL and CESC.

2.7.3 Delays in approval of DPRs

The DPRs for RGGVY were to be prepared in compliance with the detailed
guidelines formulated (June 2005) by REC, which is the central agency for
approval of the DPRs and release of central funds. Test check showed that
REC rejected (December 2005 to March 2007) eight DPRs prepared in respect
of eight districts, whose details are given in Annexure 2.1. They were finally
approved in April 2008 after a delay of up to 27 months.

Audit observed that the delays in approvals of DPRs were due to inclusion of
cost of service connection charges for APL RHHs, inclusion of 63 KVA
distribution transformers (DTRs) instead of within 25 KVA, incomplete
information mentioned in the DPRs, wide variation in cost of electrification
per village ranging between हैं 6.40 lakh and & 40.26 lakh against an average of

16 Calculation was based on the average cost of electrification per village in the respective

% 7.61 lakh ultimately approved etc. These had resulted in delays in issue of
tenders, award of contracts to the contractors and finally execution of projects
and delivery of benefits to the target beneficiaries. Detail is given in
Annexure 2.2.

Management replied that delay in approval of DPRs was due to efforts made
for inclusion of more BPL RHHs and infrastructure under the scheme. Audit,
however, observed that reasons as mentioned above were, in fact, more
relevant.

2.7.4 Inflated budgets in the DPRs

State Government had exempted (June 2006) material inputs for RGGVY
from sales tax. However, test check by audit revealed that the DPRs submitted
for RGGVY mentioned the cost of material inclusive of sales tax.

Similarly, RGGVY guidelines required that cost of individual items should not
include sundry charges'” while calculating project cost. The DPRs submitted
for RGGVY, however, were found to have included sundry charges.

Thus, inclusion of these items in the DPR was against the prescribed norms
and hence resulted in enhancing the cost by हैं 37.79 crore in case of the
10 projects.

Management stated (November 2013) that it was the standard norm of
WBSEDCL to prepare estimates at landed cost of materials inclusive of taxes
and duties as well as to add sundries to the supply cost of materials.

Reply is not acceptable as the projects were being undertaken under the
RGGVY and since specific directions/ guidelines for RGGVY were available,
the same should have been applied while preparing DPRs of these projects
instead of following the standards norms of WBSEDCL.

2.8 Contract Management

Contract Management involves preparation of realistic estimates, public
notification of tenders and selection and award of tenders in competitive and
transparent manner. During implementation of RGGVY, WBSEDCL had to
undertake construction works where private contractors were involved. The
audit observations in this respect are given in the succeeding paragraphs.

2.8.1 Inflated estimates

As mentioned in Paragraph 2.7.4, DPR budget included cost towards sales tax
and sundries. These costs were also included in the estimates
prepared (April 2008) and put to tender for execution of the works under
RGGVY. The estimates further included ‘overhead charges’ which were in

"7 All the items necessary for creation of infrastructure were included at the rate of five per
cent in sundries.

22


Chapter II Performance audit relating to Government Company

the nature of administrative costs to be retained by WBSEDCL and
‘franchisee charges’ to be paid to the franchisees'*. This led to over
estimation of costs to the extent of हैं 62.36 crore” in the estimates put to
tender.

Management reiterated (November 2013) that it was the standard norm of
WBSEDCL to prepare estimates at landed cost of materials inclusive of taxes
and duties as well as to add sundries with supply cost of materials. Besides,
overhead and franchisee charges were included to arrive at the tentative unit
cost of any job for comparison with the lowest rate quoted for that work.

However, elements of sales tax and sundries charges were not to be included
in the estimate as per REC’s observation, 2006, as already stated. Franchisee
charges were to be paid to the franchisee to be appointed for revenue
collection only after creation of the infrastructure whereas overhead charges
which were in the nature of administrative charges were to be retained by the
implementing agency and not to be included in the estimates and put to
tenders.

2.8.2 Lack of prudence in award of tenders

Audit observed that WBSEDCL had divided the total work under RGGVY
into two or three packages in all the test checked districts, during January/
February 2009, on turnkey basis by inviting open tenders. Audit noticed that
the specification of the materials used like PCC poles, ACSR conductors,
Distribution transformers etc. was alike in all the works. The ex-works” rates
of these materials were, however, different for packages in the same district.
Details of impact of ex-works cost on all works are given in Annexure 2.3.
Allowing different rates for the same materials in different packages in the
same districts by the same contractor resulted in extra expenditure of
= 7.86 crore in nine packages.

Similarly, it was seen that works were awarded (January/ February 2009) in
adjoining districts and that the ex-works rates of the same materials were
different for packages. Details of impact of % 11.06 crore on ex-works cost is
given in Annexure 2.4. Allowing different rates for the same material
resulted in extra expenditure of = 18.92 crore (® 7.86 crore + हैं 11.06 crore) in
these cases.

Management stated in September 2013 that the electrification work under
RGGVY scheme was divided into packages in different districts and each
package had been considered as one project and tenders for such packages/
projects had been invited on turnkey concept. Different rates were quoted in
tenders by different agencies for different locations in a district for a particular

18 Franchisees would be appointed for billing and revenue collection on creation of
infrastructure and energisation of BPL RHHs.

19 Due to inclusion of sales tax (€ 14.32 crore), sundries (€ 23.47crore), franchisees charges
0.52 crore) and overhead charges (€ 24.05 crore).

20 Ex-works rate excludes all taxes, duties, freight and insurance.

23


Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

item of supply which was acceptable in turnkey contracts. Management
further added (November 2013) that each package was put to tender as a
separate project on turnkey concept.

However, audit had examined the contract documents and found that the
tender documents were made item wise and the payments were also released
item wise.

2.8.3 Failure to safeguard WBSEDCL’s interest

Mobilisation advance is released to a contractor for mobilising materials and
labour at the work site and is recovered within a fixed time frame irrespective
of the progress of the work.

The terms of tender notified (April/ September 2008) for RGGVY contracts
allowed for release of mobilisation advance of 15 per cent and 10 per cent
respectively on supply and erection of the components. There was, however,
no stipulation of interest chargeable on the advance and there was no time
frame fixed for the recovery of these advances. WBSEDCL had disbursed
(April 2009 to March 2011) % 145.43 crore as interest free mobilisation
advance to the contractors against 23 packages.

These advances were adjusted at the rate of “10 per cent in case of erection”
and “15 per cent in case of supply of materials” from the bills submitted by
the contractors. The initial completion target for these 23 packages was
August 2010 but the work had not been completed till March 2013. As of
March 2013, mobilisation advance of € 19.63 crore remained unadjusted with
nine contractors implementing 17 packages, resulting in blocking up of funds
for a period ranging from 21 months to 31 months with consequent loss of
interest” of € 1.51 crore.

Management observed (November 2013) that the guideline” issued (February
2008) by the Central Vigilance Commission (CVC) stated that decision to
stipulate interest free mobilisation advance in the tender document rests on the
level of Board (with concurrence of Finance) in the organisations and the
Letters of Award (LOAs) had been awarded after approval from the Board
Committee.

The CVC guidelines, while delegating the decision to stipulate interest free
mobilisation advance, required clear stipulation in the tender document for
time based recovery not linked to the progress of work to reduce scope of
misuse of such advance by the contractor.

2.8.4 Non-installation of pilfer-proof (PP) meter box

RGGVY guidelines provide for provision of service connections at a cost of
₹ 1,900 per BPL RHH_ including materials supply, erection and
commissioning. It also specifies procurement of goods and services as per
REC Manual. REC Manual” prescribes service connections to consumers

2181 3.5 per cent per annum for 21 to 31 months.
22 Circular No. 5/2/2008, OM No. 4CC-1-CTE-2 dated 05 February 2008.
2

4/1975.

24


Chapter II Performance audit relating to Government Company

should be made theft proof. Since 2007-08, WBSEDCL has been installing
pilfer proof (PP) meter boxes procured at हैं 93.81 each, when providing new
connections to consumers (including BPL RHHs). But while floating tenders
for service connections under RGGVY in April/ September 2008, WBSEDCL
had tendered for service connections including supply of meters but without
indicating requirement of PP meter boxes.

The contractors supplied and installed service connections without PP meter
boxes at % 1,900 per connection. Thus, award of work for installation at
% 1,900 per connection without inclusion of PP meter box as stipulated in the
guidelines, resulted in extra benefit of € 9.30 crore (% 93.81 per PP meter box
for 9.91 lakh service connections effected in 10 districts) to contractors and
also put the meters at risk of being tampered.

Management contended (November 2013) that REC guideline had nowhere
required inclusion of pilfer proof boxes in the erection charges of service
connections.

The contention overlooked the fact that REC Manual requires service connections
to be made theft proof which is done by installing meters in PP boxes.

2.8.5 Excess payment of tax

The terms and conditions of the tender provided that price bids were to be
quoted exclusive of all taxes and duties viz., sales tax, excise duty, local taxes,
service tax as well as other taxes and levies applicable in West Bengal. All
applicable taxes and duties were to be indicated separately in the bid. Further,
as per LOA, WBSEDCL shall have to pay or realise from the contractors any
upward/ downward variations in rates of excise duty, sales tax etc. on direct
items prevailing at the time of delivery.

However Audit observed that in 10 out of 24 packages, sales tax at the rate of
two to four per cent was included in ex-works price. It was observed that
despite sales tax not being applicable in RGGVY, WBSEDCL paid
% 8.32 crore (Annexure 2.5) towards sales tax on the above packages. REC
did not reimburse amount of sales tax paid by WBSEDCL as it was not
indicated separately in the bid as required under RGGVY guidelines.

Moreover in six out of 24 packages, excise duty at the then applicable rate was
included in the bids. However at the time of supply of materials rate of excise
duty had reduced. WBSEDCL did not get the advantage of reduced excise
duty and paid the contractors रैं 97.40 lakh towards higher excise duty
mentioned in the bids as per details given in Annexure 2.6.

Similarly, in eight out of 24 packages, the contractor submitted price bids for
erection and energisation inclusive of taxes and duties at the then prevailing
rate. During execution the rate of service tax had reduced, but WBSEDCL
paid service tax to the contractors at quoted rate without considering the lower
rate prevailing at the time of execution. As a result, excess payment of
% 74.79 lakh towards service tax as given in Annexure 2.7 was made to the
contractors.

25


Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

WBSEDCL admitted and stated (April 2013) that recovery of the excess
amount of tax would be effected. However, no recoveries had been made till
the close of audit (August 2013).

2.9 Project execution

Project execution requires a close vigil over the progress of the work ensuring
that it is executed as envisaged in the DPR, within the stipulated time frame to
the fulfilment of the scheme objectives.

2.9.1 Delay in completion of works

Initially, the projects under RGGVY were to be completed by June 2010.
These were delayed up to 27 months in obtaining approval for the DPRs from
REC. After the DPRs were approved, the tenders for execution of the works
under RGGVY were notified in April/ September 2008 and letters of
awards (LOA) for execution were issued in January/ February 2009 and
December 2010 after competitive bidding.

The LOAs required the execution of the projects to be completed by
July/ August 2010/ June 2011 i.e. 18 months from date of award. However,
out of eight districts scrutinised by audit, work in only one district was
completed as of March 2013.

eight districts:-

Management agreed that there were delays and stated (November 2013) that

from District authorities, difficulty in pole procurement due to a rush of orders

to the PCC pole manufacturers, and obstruction raised by the villagers during

capacity DTR, three phase L.T O.H line and land dispute for erection of poles.

Audit, however, observed from the minutes of review meetings and reply of
the Project Manager that the slow progress of the projects was due to other
reasons such as “delay of mobilisation of materials”, “failure of material
procurement plan by turnkey contractor”, “delay in supply of material”, “less
engagement of manpower for field activities” (erection of poles, stringing of
conductors as well as providing BPL service connections). A letter issued by
the Chief Engineer (RE) in April 2011 also attributed the delay to “lack of co-
ordination between the contractors and WBSEDCL” and “passive attitude
towards electrification of BPLHH by RE and Operations and
Maintenance (O&M) wing of WBSEDCL”.

2.9.2 Non-levy of liquidated damages

All contracts awarded for RGGVY works had provision to levy liquidated
damages (LD) for delay in completion of the project. LD was to be charged at
0.5 per cent per week up to a maximum of five per cent of the total value of
the contract. Due to delay in execution of the project in 10 districts where
RGGVY had been executed by WBSEDCL, ¥ 57.86 crore in LD was leviable
but not levied by WBSEDCL.

Management while accepting the audit observation stated (November 2013)
that LD would be finalised at the time of closure of contracts. It further
informed that provisional deductions were now being made from the
contractors’ bills.

2.9.3 Non-provision of connections to BPL RHHs

The BPL RHHs were to be provided with service connection and energized
under the RGGVY scheme within August 2010. However, audit observed”*
that in 217 villages of six districts’> inspite of creating the requisite
infrastructure (like lines and installation of DTRs) at a cost of € 6.25 crore and
declaration of energisation of villages between April 2010 to January 2013,
WBSEDCL could not provide service connection to 15,433 BPL RHHs.
Management in their reply (November 2013) admitted that in some districts
the number of BPL connection effected was far less than the LOA quantity
and attributed this to different reasons like ROW” problem, connection
already effected and beyond infrastructure. However, ROW problem would
not be relevant as infrastructure had already been created.

26 Right Of Way

2.9.4 Creation of infrastructure backbone

For creation of Rural Electricity Distribution Backbone (REDB), RGGVY
envisaged village electrification infrastructure and rural household
electrification of BPL RHHs free of cost. However, audit found some
deficiencies in creation of infrastructure.

2.9.4.1 Inadequate distribution transformer capacity

In the eight districts, total of 18.59 lakh RHHs (BPL: 13.66 lakh,
APL : 4.93 lakh) were proposed to be electrified as per DPR. WBSEDCL
considered only 11 watt (0.006875 KVA) load per BPL household against a
load of 60 watt (0.0375 KVA) specified in RGGVY guideline. It ignored load
requirement of 500 watts (0.3125 KVA) meant to be provided to each
APL RHHs as per guidelines. Moreover, under the guidelines, DTRs should
also have loads up to a maximum of 90 per cent of their capacity, or
228.148 MVA. However, WBSEDCL had fixed load targets of only
183.605 MVA. WBSEDCL stated (November 2013) that capacity of DTRs
was selected considering estimated load to BPL RHHs only.

In the minutes of capital work progress review meetings of WBSEDCL held in
August, September and October 2011, it was stated by management that large
number of DTRs were burnt due to overloading at places where RGGVY
works were completed with transformers having lower load capacity that
could not cope up with higher loads actually drawn by the consumers. It was
seen that 559 out of 2,571 DTRs were burnt out. In September 2011,
WBSEDCL decided that these burnt DTR would be repaired and would be
utilised elsewhere. It was further decided that O&M wing of WBSEDCL
would assess the number of consumers and their load and if the capacity of
DTR was found insufficient to meet total requirement, then higher capacity
DTR would be installed. WBSEDCL had itself noticed that there were
instances of hooking also which increased the load further on the DTRs.

Avoidable repair cost incurred on DTRs was हैं 53 lakh. Management did not
contest the audit observation, and it was seen by audit that in September 2011
WBSEDCL itself had decided to reassess the number of consumers and the
higher load requirement in order to augment the capacity of DTRs.

Subsequently, in seven districts, 722DTRs of 10KVA load each were
replaced by 25 KVA or 63 KVA DTRs; however, the contractors had not
returned the 10 KVA DTRs and associated equipment worth = 55.80 lakh to
WBSEDCL so far (October 2013). It was also seen that in 164 DTRs out of
these 722 cases, the DTR energy accounting meters had become non-
functional since they were not compatible with higher capacity DTRs.

2.9.5 Scheme impact

Once requisite infrastructure has been created and BPL RHHs were provided
with connections, RE wing of WBSEDCL hands over the infrastructure to
O&M wing of WBSEDCL which energises the lines and arranges for delivery

28


Chapter II Performance audit relating to Government Company

of energy to HH. It is also responsible for billing and realisation of revenue
from the consumers. Audit scrutiny of billing process revealed the following:

e WBSEDCL had resorted to minimum billing of हैं 38 per month (minimum
consumption charges) per consumer in respect of 2,941 BPL consumers
for three to 24 months the total consumption charges of € 20.17 lakh were
not realised from the RHHs which were ‘untraceable’.

e WBSEDCL had raised bills on 11,898 BPL consumers, who were not even
provided with any electricity connection.

e As per orders of Chief Engineer, RE, WBSEDCL (April 2010) every
connection had to be energised within 15 days of completion of
infrastructure. In six districts, 2,412 villages where infrastructure was
provided with energy connections to BPL RHHs were given after a delay
of 16 to 829 days.

e WBSEDCL is to raise bills on BPL RHHs once every quarter as per Tariff
Order of the WBSEDCL. However, meter reading of 3.51 lakh BPL

e But it was seen that in respect of 1.98 lakh BPL RHHs, first bill had been

defeating the very objective of the scheme. Besides, bills had not yet been
raised on 10,354 BPL RHHs even after delays from one to three years
since connections were given.

Management had accepted (November 2013) audit findings.
2.10 Financial operations

RGGVY scheme is financed by Gol (90 per cent of project cost) through REC
and 10 per cent contributed by the State Government through own resources.
Out of total project cost of हैं 1,239.95 crore sanctioned (January 2009) to
WBSEDCL for implementation of scheme in respect of provision of
connections to BPL RHHs, entire expenditure was to be covered by way of
90 per cent capital subsidy and 10 per cent loan. The financial assistance was
to be released in four instalments depending on the progress of work. REC
had released रैं 997.98 crore to WBSEDCL between February 2009 and March
2012 against which total expenditure up to March 2013 was & 923.63 crore
(93 per cent) (refer to Table 2.1).

29


Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

As per REC circular issued in April 2008, funds provided to WBSEDCL were
required to be retained in a dedicated interest-bearing bank account and
interest earned thereon till March 2013 was to be ploughed back into RGGVY
and to be adjusted against the last instalment. Audit scrutiny revealed that on
receipt of first instalment of हैं 382.47 crore in February/ March 2009,
WBSEDCL delayed opening of separate bank account for RGGVY funds by
nine to 25 days, till which time the funds were lying with WBSEDCL without
earning any interest. As a result interest amounting to € 0.39 crore could not
be earned and credited to the RGGVY account. Besides, % 5.86 crore
earmarked for RGGVY was retained in corporate account of WBSEDCL
(2009-2013) instead of the dedicated account of RGGVY which deprived it of
हैं 1.99 crore as interest. However, at the instance of audit WBSEDCL
reconciled it in 2013 and transferred it to its dedicated account.

Management stated (November 2013) that delay in opening a new account
was due to processing time and retention of RGGVY funds in non-designated
account was attributed to non-availability of release advices initially.

But, management was well aware of the fact that a separate dedicated account
was to be opened for RGGVY fund, which was notified in April 2008.
Further, due to periodical non-reconciliation of RGGVY funds received from
REC from time to time, Management was unable to trace the funds kept in
separate account other than the dedicated RGGVY account.

2.11 Project monitoring

Success of any project depends on timely and effective monitoring. The
monitoring of RGGVY projects were at the level of WBSEDCL and REC.
While REC appointed (January 2009) a private company as third party
inspection agency (TPIA), WBSEDCL had appointed (June/ August 2009)
REC Power Distribution Company Ltd. and RITES for the same purpose.

2.11.1 Belated inspection

The TPIAs inspected 4,939 villages between November 2010 and February
2013 and had reported 42,677 defects.”’ Although all these defects were
required to be rectified within one month as per the LOA, only 12,679 defects
were rectified till March 2013. At SMP, Birbhum and Nadia, 96, 41 and
30 villages out of 127, 91 and 124 villages respectively were inspected by the
TPIAs after completion of guarantee period of one year. This had defeated the
purpose of inspection in rectification of the defects free of cost by the
contractors.

Management agreed (November 2013) with the audit observation.

*"The nature of defects included improper binding, loose stay, tilted pole, absence of tree
clearance etc.

30


Chapter II Performance audit relating to Government Company

2.11.2 Meetings of monitoring committees

Gol had directed (May 2008) that WBSEDCL must regularly monitor the
physical and financial progress of work by holding bi-monthly meetings of
State Level Co-ordination Committee (SLCC). GoWB had constituted the
SLCC to monitor the implementation of the scheme in December 2008.
However, during the period 2008-2013, only sixteen SLCC meetings were
held against the required number of 26.

Similarly, District Level Co-ordination Committees (DLCCs), constituted in
May 2009, were to meet once a month. Against 48 meetings required to be
held in each district during the period 2009-13, 12 to 27 meetings were held
during the same period in all eight districts.

2.11.3 Non-receipt of village electrification certificates

After electrification of each village under RGGVY, a village certificate was to
be issued by the Gram Panchayat (G.P.) indicating the date of electrification,
number of BPL RHHs provided with electricity connections and the number
and aggregate capacity of DTR installed in the village.

Till March 2013, out of 12,594 villages reported as electrified, village
electrification certificate was available for only 3,572 villages (28 per cent).
The district-wise numbers of panchayat certificates received are shown in the
table below (Table 2.5) from which it will be seen that in two districts, more
than 90 per cent of the certificates were received (August 2013):

The reason for less receipt of panchayat certificates was due to Gram

November 2013, however, this was still less than 50 per cent of villages

Conclusion

DPRs were prepared without preparing the RE plan or conducting
field surveys. Consequently, BPL HHs had been omitted from the
DPRs, while villages not requiring or facing statutory/ physical
constraints in electrification had been included in the DPRs.

Tenders were floated without inserting clauses to ensure
reasonability of prices of materials to be supplied leading to
varying ex-works prices for the same materials supplied by the
same contractor in different packages; while excise duty, sales tax
and service tax were paid in excess of the prevailing rates in
deviation of tender provisions. Execution of works and creation of
infrastructure had been delayed on account of belated delivery of
materials, inadequate deployment of manpower, lack of co-
ordination, etc leading to shortfall in the numbers of BPL RHHs
electrified. Besides, liquidated damages were not imposed on the
contractors.

Although adequate fund were received, the progress of works was
not commensurate with disbursements. Further, the receipt of
funds had not been reconciled.

Monitoring was ineffective with belated third party inspection.

Recommendations

DPRs to be prepared with proper field survey.

Appropriate clauses to be incorporated in contracts to safeguard the
interest of WBSEDCL.

Applicability of correct rate of taxes and duties be ensured.

System of monitoring needs to be strengthened.

Chapter III

Performance audit relating to Statutory Corporation

West Bengal Financial Corporation

Financial Assistance to entrepreneurs and recovery of loans

Executive Summary

Introduction

West Bengal Financial Corporation
(WBFO) was set up in 1954 under the State
Financial Corporations Act, 1951. It is the
key state level lending institution for the
Micro, Small and Medium
Enterprises (MiSME), with a Head Office at
Kolkata, Regional Offices at Siliguri,
Durgapur and Kolkata and 11 Branch
Offices throughout the State.

WBFC is managed by a Board of Directors
consisting of 10 Directors _ including
Managing Director and four directors
nominated by financial institutions. The
Managing Director of WBFC is the chief
executive.

Operational and financial performance

Despite being the key state level lending
institution for financing MiSME, WBFC
financed only 2.10 per cent of new units that
had come up in last five years. WBFC
became increasingly dependent on bonds as
SIDBI, being its source of finance had
gradually phased out re-finance loans.
WBFC’s equity base of %147.35 crore was
almost wiped out due to accumulated losses
of €128.42 crore. Its non-performing assets
remained above 26 per cent at the end of
2012-13. It had to bear avoidable interest
burden due to absence of proper planning
for redemption of bonds.

Sanction and disbursement of loan

WBFC had framed (February 2008) an
entrepreneur evaluation policy which
seemed to discourage sanction of loan to
first time entrepreneurs. WBFC also did not
subscribe to the Credit Guarantee Fund

Trust Scheme for Micro and Small
Enterprises (MiSE). This was another
obstacle for new MiSEs to receive loans.

WBFC did not prepare industry-wise
appraisal manual. It did not rate credit risk
or subscribe to Credit Information Bureau
(India) Limited for credit history of
borrowers until January 2013.

Instances of deficient appraisal, absence of
due diligence, incorrect assessment of
working capital and acceptance of flawed
security in appraisal, sanction and
disbursement of loans were noticed in audit.

Audit also observed high concentration of
credit to defaulting sectors and loans to
ineligible borrowers.

Monitoring and recovery performance

WBEC had adopted a monitoring and follow
up guideline in January 2008. However, it
did not ask for progress report of the
projects or the annual accounts from the
borrowers. Low number of inspections
coupled with failure to verify books of
accounts of borrowers resulted in default in
repayment of loans.

WBEC failed to invoke the provisions of the
State Financial Corporations Act, 1951 as
also adopt the procedures specified in the
recovery manual to promptly recover its
dues from defaulting borrowers.

Internal Control and Internal Audit

Allocation of duties and responsibilities was
diffused. Besides, the requirement of having
updated information on borrowers as per
‘Know your client’ prescribed by Reserve
Bank was not followed.

Conclusion and Recommendations

WBFC was not a significant source of
financing MiSME units in the State. Its
appraisal mechanism was inadequate.
WBFC did not follow its own monitoring

processes were weak. strengthen its appraisal mechanism and
ensure greater compliance with its

Government may review the objectives and
3.1 Introduction

West Bengal Financial Corporation (WBFC) was set up in 1954 under
Section 3(1) of the State Financial Corporations Act, 1951 (the Act). Its
principal objectives were to promote and develop industrial growth in the State
by providing financial assistance to Micro, Small and Medium
Enterprises (MiSMEs). WBFC is the key state level lending institution for
MiSMEs under the control of the Finance Department, Government of West
Bengal. It has its head office at Kolkata, three Regional Offices at Siliguri,
Durgapur and Kolkata and 11 Branch Offices' throughout the State.

A Performance Audit on the “Development of SSI Units in West Bengal” by
WBEFC was incorporated in the Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General
of India for the year ended 31 March 2001 (Commercial), Government of
West Bengal (GoWB).

3.2 Organisation Structure

Management of WBFC is vested in a Board of Directors (BoD) consisting of
10 Directors (as of March 2013) including the Managing Director (MD) and
four Directors nominated by three financial institutions’. MD of WBFC is
chief executive working under superintendence of the BoD. There is an
Executive Committee constituted by the Board in terms of Section 18 of the
Act and other Committees constituted under Section 21 of the Act, for
discharging functions as prescribed / delegated by Board for the purpose of
assisting it for efficient discharge of its functions.

3.3 Audit Objectives

As per XI Plan of the State Government, MiSME is to play a pivotal role in
industrial resurgence of the country. At present over 90 per cent of industrial
units in the State are in micro and small scale industrial sector. This sector
provides the second highest employment opportunities to people after
agriculture sector. Nearly 60 per cent of industrial output in the State is from
cottage and small scale industries and it accounts for 50 per cent of the State’s
overall exports. This sector also provides the first generation entrepreneurs
their first exposure to entrepreneurship and industrial activities. However,
despite being identified as a priority sector for lending by the Reserve Bank of
India (२81), business suffers from shortage of finance from time to time.

1 Coochbehar, Raigunj, Malda, Berhampore, Suri, Purulia, Bankura, Mednipur, Barasat,
Krishnanagar and Chinsurah.

2 State Bank of India, Small Industries Development Bank of India and Life Insurance
Corporation of India.

34


Chapter III Performance audit relating to Statutory Corporation

In the recent past Commercial Banks have forayed into this sector with greater
resources and diversified products at their disposal. WBFC’s performance had
increasingly conceded its competitive space to the Commercial Banks and its
profitability has been on the decline.

In order to understand why WBFC was losing its market share and what
factors affected its operations and resultant profitability, performance audit
was undertaken with following objectives:

. Whether operations and financial performances of WBFC were
profitable and fulfilled the objective of promoting MiSMEs in the State;

. Whether policies adopted by WBFC were adequate and procedures for
appraisal, sanction and disbursement of loans were effective;

. Whether processes for monitoring and timely recovery of loans were
efficient; and

. Whether an adequate system of internal control was in place and was
operative.

3.4 Scope and Methodology of Audit

Performance Audit was undertaken between February and June 2013. It
covered activities of WBFC with regard to financial assistance extended
during 2008-09 to 2012-13. Records at the head office of WBFC and four’ out
of 14 field offices were test checked by audit. Selection of units was done on
the basis of random sampling method of probability proportionate to size
without replacement (PPSWOR). Size-measure was the aggregate principal
outstanding as at 31 December 20124 against loans disbursed. In addition
Siliguri Branch cum Regional Office, was included in the sample on
suggestion of the management due to the office having large number of
borrowers.

Audit methodology included scrutiny of loan files, cases of compromise
settlement and sale of assets, analysis of WBFC’s Annual Reports and SQL
queries upon the WBFC’s database. Audit also examined correspondence and
directives from the State Government and Small Industries Development Bank
of India (51081), minutes and agenda papers of the Board meetings of WBFC.

Audit explained its objectives, criteria, scope and methodology to WBFC
during an ‘Entry Conference’ held on 4 April 2013. The draft report was
discussed in the ‘Exit Conference’ held on 19 November 2013 which was
attended by the Managing Director and senior management of WBFC.

3 Two regional offices at Kolkata and Durgapur which function as larger branch offices and
two smaller branch offices at Midnapore and Barasat.
+ At the time of drawing of sample in March 2013, data up to December 2012 were available.

35


Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

3.5 Audit Criteria

WBFC’s performance was assessed with respect to the following:
. State Financial Corporations Act, 1951;

e Laid down procedures for sanction, disbursement, monitoring and
recovery along with policies on resource mobilisation; and

. Tripartite Memorandum of Understanding between State Government,
SIDBI and WBFC.

Audit Findings

3.6 Operational and Financial Performance

WBEFEC operates as a prime lending institution of the State Government for

financing micro, small and medium scale industries and services. The

enterprises, categorised under micro, small or medium scale in terms of
investment are depicted in the following flow chart.

For extending sustained financial help to such sectors for industrial
development, WBFC needs to have sound financial health with prudent
business plans. However, Audit observed that WBFC could neither meet
increasing demand for loans by Micro and Small enterprises (MiSE) sector nor
were its operations profitable, as discussed in the succeeding paragraphs.

The growth of new MiSEs (excluding medium size industries) set up and those

The table shows that number of units financed by WBFC had actually fallen

of volume of financing, total amount disbursed increased at a compound
annual average rate of only 7.5 per cent over the five years period. Thus it is
seen that WBFC could hardly contribute to the growth of MiSMEs within the
State during five years as its financing either in terms of quantum of loans
disbursed or in attracting more MiSME units for financing had remained
virtually stagnant over this period. Audit observed that failure of WBFC to
expand its level of activity and thereby promote the growth of this sector can
be attributed to a variety of structural and operational deficiencies as explained
in the subsequent paragraphs.

3.6.2 Financial Performance

WBFC prepares annual Business Plan and Resource Forecast (BPRF) at the
behest of SIDBI wherein it sets targets for its loan sanction and disbursement.
It submits BPRF to SIDBI stating extent of loan sought from SIDBI, who
sanctions loans based on its availability of funds and WBFC raises remaining
funds through issue of bonds. Accordingly, WBFC issues bonds through

37


Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

private placement with competitive interest rates prevailing in the market.
These bonds carry a maturity period of eight/ ten years with ‘put and call’
option for their redemption at the end of six/ eight years. Besides these two
sources of financing, it also generates internal resources from recovery of past
loans and also from its revenues that comprise interest receipts on loans and
other receipts like interest on deposits and miscellaneous income. It also
receives subsidies from Central and State Governments. Finally it takes
overdrafts from banks from time to time to fill any gap in its financing
requirements.

Respective shares of resources raised from different sources by WBFC during
the last five years are shown in Table 3.3 from which it is seen that proportion
of resources raised from SIDBI had declined from 31.06 per cent in 2008-09 to
only 10.34 per cent in 2012-13, which had to be made up by corresponding
increases in share of resources raised through bonds. In 2010-11, it did not issue
any bond since substantial resources were generated from recovery of loans
@ 142.18 crore), which was the case in 2011-12 also, though bonds had to be
issued in that year to compensate for the steep decline in SIDBI financing.
However, in 2012-13, even though = 172.49 crore came from recovery of past
loans, the net internal resources after repayment of loans to SIDBI became
negative and WBFC had to issue fresh bonds for % 224.88 crore for raising
required resources.

This shows that there was no consistent pattern in raising of loans and their
repayment. An efficient system of managing assets and liabilities includes
creation of adequate quality assets to meet liabilities towards long-term
borrowings. WBFC has, however, not planned for redemption of bonds and
repayment of loans from SIDBI by way of measures such as regularly setting
aside fund to avoid putting its finances to stress.

Audit analysis of WBFC’s financing decisions revealed that due to absence of
a system of asset liability management, WBFC had to obtain funds from
costlier sources for redemption of its bonds at maturity and exposed itself to
avoidable risks as discussed in the following paragraphs.

3.6.2.1 Extra interest burden on costlier loans

As a lending institution, WBFC should procure funds from cheapest sources
and ensure matching of the tenure of loans given to borrowers with tenure of
its sources. WBFC’s long term sources of funds were either from bonds or
Normal Refinance Scheme!! (NRS) loans from SIDBI. The tenure of both
these sources is seven/ eight years'* which matched average tenure of loans
sanctioned by WBFC. Short term requirements are met through short term
loans from SIDBI for period upto three years under the Special Refinance
Scheme (SRS) of SIDBI.

The Chart 3.1 below shows the comparative costs of long term loans during
the period from 2008-09 to 2012-13.
