It is seen that except in 2011-12, SIDBI financing was always costlier
compared to financing through bonds. This resulted in extra interest burden of
% 1.34 crore as of 31 March 2013 due to higher costs of NRS loans'* from
SIDBI. Besides, WBFC would carry interest burden of रैं 87.83 lakh on these
SIDBI loans till their complete liquidation at maturity.

In reply to audit query, management stated (July 2013) lower drawal of NRS
loans from SIDBI in any particular year would affect NRS loans in subsequent
years. However, management added (October 2013) that SIDBI indicated its
intention to reduce loan limits progressively and intended to liquidate the
entire outstanding within 2022.

Thus on one hand WBFC mentioned its compulsion of availing NRS loan
from SIDBI to avoid reduction of NRS loans in future, on the other hand
indicated SIDBI’s intention to reduce loan limits progressively and liquidate
the entire outstanding by 2022. It is clear from the reply that SIDBI loans
would be reduced over time irrespective of quantum of loan availed annually
by WBFC in previous year.

3.6.2.2 Avoidable interest due to absence of proper planning

Due to its failure to set aside fund for redemption of its bonds, WBFC was
unable to insulate itself against risks due to fluctuation in market rate of
interest and also to take advantage of the falling rate of interest in the market
as discussed in following paragraphs.

© In 2008-09, WBFC’s cost of funds was 8.98 per cent’ which further
declined to 8.60 percent in 2009-10. In 2008-09, WBFC had
% 40.24 crore worth of bonds maturing in 2010-11 carrying interest rate
at 9.50 per cent which was higher than prevailing market rate of
interest. But WBFC in absence of fund at its disposal, could not call
back these high cost bonds from the market by exercising call option
and thus substitute these high cost bonds with lower-cost bonds at
prevailing lower market rates. The high cost bonds could only be
redeemed in 2010-11. In the process, WBFC lost opportunity to save
interest of € 31.90 lakh during 2008-09 and 2009-10.

e Again, during 2011-12 WBFC raised हैं 72.55 crore by issuing bonds
redeemable in 2020-21 at an average cost of 9.56 per cent and used
part of these proceeds to repay % 42.38 crore of bonds which had
average cost of 8.19 per cent as it had no resources at its disposal to
redeem them. Thus low cost bonds were redeemed with funds from

34 Loans are repaid in equal instalments across seven years.
15 Calculated by the weighted average interest rate of bonds issued during the year.

40


Chapter III Performance audit relating to Statutory Corporation

high cost bonds and WBFC was burdened with avoidable additional
annual interest burden of % 58.16 lakh till 2020-21.

e Similarly, during 2012-13, WBFC issued % 224.88 crore in bonds
redeemable in 2021-22 at interest rates ranging between 9.52 per cent
to 9.70 per cent, part of which was used to redeem bonds of € 47 crore
which carried interest at 7.75 per cent to 8.50 per cent. As a result,
WBFC was burdened with additional annual interest costs of
& 81.78 lakh till 2021-22.

Management replied (October 2013) that fund set aside to repay borrowings,
can be utilised for specific purpose only and therefore would remain idle.
Further, liquidity had been maintained by investing surplus fund in fixed
deposit receipts to fetch handsome returns.

However, setting aside of fund does not mean that it would remain idle. They
could have been invested in instruments of adequate safety and liquidity as
well as matching with tenure for repayment of borrowings.

3.6.2.3 Financial results and profitability

Some indicators of financial performance of WBFC for last five years ending
2012-13 are given in Table 3.4. Working results and financial position during
these years are given in Annexure 3.1. From Table 3.4, it is seen that the
debt equity ratio of WBFC had increased from 3.94 per cent to 4.78 per cent
over this period, indicating increasing interest charges that was affecting its
profits negatively. While capital employed increased by 42 per cent during
the period, return on capital employed practically remained at the same level.
Net worth of WBFC also remained at the same level during the last four years.
Except in 2010-11, the WBFC did not earn any profit, yet paid dividends in
2010-11 as well as 2011-12. At the end of 2012-13, its equity base of
% 147.35 crore had almost been wiped out by the accumulated losses of
& 128.42 crore. WBFC, however, could not bring down the share of its NPA
in total loan assets which remained above 26 per cent at the end of 2008-09,
2011-12 as well as 2012-13, which was far above the desired level of
10 per cent as dictated by the terms of MoU agreed with the SIDBI which
refinanced the loans given by WBFC.

From the expense ratios, it is seen that more than 60 per cent of its cost were only
due to interest and finance charges. WBFC raised its finances from SIDBI and
also through private placement of bonds. Employee cost which accounted for
15 per cent of total costs in 2008-09, increased to as much as 24 per cent in
2010-11 due to payment of arrears of pay revision. Administrative and general
expenses varied between four per cent and 14 per cent of total costs during the
period.

Management stated (October 2013) that it was empowered under
Section 35(2) of the Act to declare dividend out of current profit and that
decision to declare dividend was approved by the Board and shareholders.

However under the Act, WBFC could only declare dividend after making
provision for bad and doubtful debts, depreciation of assets and all other
matters which are usually provided for by banking companies. Banking
companies are prohibited” from declaring dividend until all capitalised
expenses”! have been completely written off.

Development Financial Institutions (May 2004) appointed by the Reserve
Bank of India on the future of SFCs. The report had categorically stated:

“State Financial Corporations (SFCs) had been established as state level
institutions for meeting the financial requirements of small and medium scale
industries under State Financial Corporations Act, 1951. SFCs have, however,
even after their long existence remained largely "single product" provider
extending term loans assistance to SSIs. They have not been able to diversify
products and services to ward off the competition from banks..... SFCs have
lost their relevance and banks are today in a position to perform all those
functions being done by the SFCs......In the final analysis the SFCs have
outlived their utility in the present context and should be phased out within a
definite timeframe. The credit gap, if any, created by the exit of the SFCs from
the market can be filled by banks and also by suitably repositioning SIDBI.”

In the context of WBFC’s financial performance, there is a need for a relook at
some of the structural issues as pointed in paragraph 3.6.2 above.

3.7 Appraisal, Sanction and Disbursement of loans

WBFC extends financial assistance upto 20 crore to a single borrower at
fixed interest rates ranging from 10.25 to 14 per cent per annum depending
upon the type of scheme, tenor and size of finance. A flow chart indicating the
process of sanction, disbursement and recovery of loans is indicated below:

3.7.1 Appraisal

Appraisal of any loan proposal involves an analysis of the project proposal to
assess its viability. It is also an assessment of the borrower’s ability to bring
in required fund into the project as promoter’s contribution. WBFC does not
have a defined appraisal guideline but adopts practices prevalent in the
industry. It had however adopted a lending policy that lays out in broad terms
which borrowers / industries are eligible for loan. Audit observations on the
appraisal process are discussed in the succeeding paragraphs.

43



Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

3.7.1.1 Lending policy

The lending policy of WBFC framed in January 2007 is revised annually and
approved by its BoD. The lending policy also lays down WBFC’s fair
practice norms.

Policy addresses sectoral exposure norms and prescribes credit rating policy,
entrepreneur appraisal policy, fair practice norms and puts emphasis on
developing industries in the backward regions. It also emphasises requisite
security and necessity for obtaining collaterals for sanction of loans. Audit
observed that policy was silent on financing ‘innovative schemes’ identified
by the SIDBI’. Efficacy of lending policy and its compliance are discussed in
following paragraphs.

3.7.1.2 Impediment in promoting first time entrepreneurs

WBEFC had put in place (February 2008) an entrepreneur evaluation policy for
fresh borrowers whereby it assigns grade points to criteria in respect of each of
the attributes like experience, net worth of promoters and prior exposure to
institutional finance (Annexure 3.2). Attributes are then assigned the
following weightages : 40 per cent for prior business experience, 40 per cent
for net worth of promoters and 20 percent for previous exposure to
institutional finance and weighted total is calculated for all attributes. A
borrower qualifies if he scores 50 per cent of the maximum grade points.

Since the borrower must obtain a minimum qualifying weighted grade point of
50 per cent, an entrepreneur desiring to undertake a business venture for the
first time would not qualify for loan from WBFC as his grade point under
prior business experience and exposure to institutional finance would be ‘nil’.

Thus, WBFC’s entrepreneur evaluation policy seems to discourage promotion
of first time entrepreneurs.

Management stated (October 2013) that the entrepreneur evaluation policy
was approved by Board comprising experienced Banking officials as well as
Government representatives. It also stated that WBFC was not averse to
provide loan assistance to new entrepreneurs within the guidelines of extant
lending policy. They further pointed out that financing new entrepreneurs was
more risky than financing their existing good clients.

However, the limitations could be mitigated by securing membership of Credit
Guarantee Fund Trust Scheme as discussed in the next section to encourage
new entrepreneurs yet some more.

22 Schemes which are energy efficient, schemes for environmental protection, etc.- Source
SIDBI Annual report 2010-11.

44


Chapter III Performance audit relating to Statutory Corporation

3.7.1.3 Credit Guarantee Fund Trust Scheme for MiSE

WBFC had not subscribed to Credit Guarantee Fund Trust Scheme for MiSE
operated by Central Government jointly with SIDBI. With subscription to this
scheme, WBFC could have provided credit facility up to % one crore without
collaterals and third-party-guarantees to MiSE purely on the primary security
of the assets financed, based on viability of a project.

Absence of subscription to Credit Guarantee Fund Trust Scheme is another
obstacle to assist first time entrepreneurs and MiSEs who may not have
adequate collaterals or obtain third party guarantee to secure a loan from
WBFC.

Management contended (October 2013) that no State Financial Corporation
was allowed to be covered under the above scheme of SIDBI.

However, State Financial Corporations of some other States (e.g. Andhra
Pradesh, Delhi and Kerala) are members of Credit Guarantee Fund Trust
Scheme. Hence, that option could have been explored by WBFC.

3.7.2 Absence of industry wise appraisal manual

Even after an existence of over sixty years, WBFC had not prepared
industry-wise appraisal manual. Although the MoU with SIDBI required
implementation of Credit Appraisal Rating Tool (CART) for loan proposals up
to % one crore, it was not implemented. WBFC did not rate credit risk of any
project and provided finance at uniform rates.

Management stated (October 2013) that it had decided not to implement
CART as its success rate was yet to be reviewed by SIDBI.

This implied that Management had agreed in MoU to implement untested
appraisal procedures. WBFC also did not subscribe”* to credit default data
from Credit Information Bureau (India) Limited (CIBIL) or similar agency
until January 2013. Consequently, it was unable to ascertain the credit history
of its borrowers.

Management stated (October 2013) that WBFC had started incorporating
default data from CIBIL in the Appraisal Memo.

3.7.3 Sanction and Disbursement of Loans

3.7.3.1 WBFC sanctions term loans as well as working capital loans to new
entrepreneurs and existing entrepreneurs on the basis of their loan proposals.
During 2008-09 to 2012-13 WBFC received 1,283 applications for loan of
₹ 1,301.05 crore of which it had approved/ sanctioned 1,239 loan applications
having financial assistance of € 1,149.02 crore as shown in Annexure 3.3.
WBFC sanctions loans for new projects as well as for expansion/

23 Tn terms of Credit Information Regulation Act, 2005.

45


Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

diversification of existing projects. During 2008-09 to 2012-13, 56 per cent of
the total loans were sanctioned for new projects and rest for diversification,
expansion and modernisation of the existing units.

3.7.3.2 Appraisal of loan involves independent assessment of the project
proposal to assess viability of project and also to ascertain whether the project
would earn adequate revenue to enable repayment of the loan along with
interest. Audit observed that WBFC’s appraisal, sanction and disbursement
mechanism seldom had independent inputs and was prepared solely based on
project reports provided by borrower. There was no prescribed methodology
to be applied in respect of such appraisal. This led to improper assessment of
viability of projects followed by a large number of default accounts which are
shown in Annexure 3.4 and discussed in the following paragraphs.

3.7.3.3 Deficient Appraisal

. For setting up of two hostels for lady boarders at Kalighat (100 beds)
and Bakul Bagan (38 beds), Kolkata, a term loan of two crore was
sanctioned (October 2009) to a firm by WBFC, Kolkata branch.

Audit observed that the appraisal on profitability of project was made on bed
strength declared by the promoter which was not assessed independently by
WBFC. After disbursement (December 2009) of € 1.20 crore, WBFC learnt
that the property at Bakul Bagan could not be purchased by promoter due to
price escalation. The loan proposal was reassessed for the hostel at Kalighat
only and was reduced to %1.60 crore. Subsequently, WBFC revised
(December 2010) the sanction to = 1.98 crore to cover additional costs of
renovation as sought by the borrower. Even at such later stage of sanction of
loan, the feasible bed strength was not assessed by WBFC. Audit further
observed that the Kalighat ladies’ hostel could only accommodate 48 beds
against 100 beds as declared by the promoter. The loan account of the said
promoter was in default as it could not service the loan with such lower
capacity. This made project unviable which in turn, rendered the loan bad.

. WBEFC, Barasat branch sanctioned (March 2009) a term loan of हैं one
crore to a firm. The project was based on client based orders placed by only
one customer. WBFC disbursed (October 2009 to March 2010) ₹ 79.97 lakh
to the borrower. However, before the project could be completed, the sole
customer of borrower shifted base to Baroda. As the product of the project
was customer specific, the project failed to take off in absence of demand.
The loan account remained in default ab-initio.

Management stated (October 2013) that it considered inputs/ data submitted
by the applicants, which were subsequently verified and validated with due
diligence during appraisal process. Besides, it had recently incorporated
improvements in the appraisal system based on SIDBI’s periodic evaluation.
However, no specific improvements were mentioned.

WBEFC did not verify the information submitted by the prospective borrowers
about their financial requirements independently from alternative sources like
suppliers of machinery efc. required to be purchased for the proposed

46


Chapter III Performance audit relating to Statutory Corporation

industrial units/ projects. Inter-firm comparisons within the same industry
were not observed in any appraisal note of the 206 sample” loan files
scrutinised by Audit.

3.7.3.4 Absence of due diligence process while appraising projects

Success of a project is dependent on availability of working capital to finance
business operations. While appraising projects, WBFC did not conduct
market survey to assess authenticity of the cost of the raw material and other
inputs. Thus viability of the projects was not assessed objectively in the first
place and requirements of working capital and long term financing could also
not be estimated correctly, which inevitably led to projects ultimately
becoming non-starters/ running into losses. As a result, borrowers could not
generate resources to repay loans and became defaulters, as discussed in the
following case studies.

3.7.3.5 Incorrect assessment of working capital

The loan sanctioned (August 2008) to a company by WBFC, Head Office, was
based on profitability assessed by considering value of raw material
consumption at 65.86 per cent of the value of output leaving a contribution
margin of 34.14 per cent with inventory, debtors and running expenses
estimated to be held for one month. Audit observed that while the project was
put into operation, actual raw material consumption to value of output worked
out to 86 per cent, while, inventory and debtors were held for four months.
This had resulted in reduction of contribution and shortage of working capital,
which made the project unviable. The account is in default due to working
capital shortage.

In respect of above case, Management stated (October 2013) that increase in
price of raw materials was due to delay in completion of project and change in
the industry norm for holding period of inventory. However, changed norm,
reasons thereof and reasons for delay were not explained.

3.7.3.6 Absence of statutory compliance

Clearances from various statutory authorities are essential for a project to take-
off. In absence of these clearances, delay in project completion and
commercial production results in fund shortage and consequently borrowers
default in repayment of interest and loan. Test check revealed two cases of
absence of statutory compliances leading to acceptance of flawed security :-

Acceptance of flawed security
. WBIC had sanctioned (October 2005) a term loan of % 4.80 crore to a

company to build a nine storied hotel with basement. The borrower had
approved plan for the said hotel from its Municipality. However, in

24 Selected as random samples out of 631 loans.

47


Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

September 2006, the borrower decided to expand its hotel to 12 floors and
submitted revised plan for sanction to the Municipality. The plan remained
unapproved till date (May 2013). Despite this, WBFC disbursed (August 2010
and July 2011) € 4.75 crore for the said project.

In absence of sanctioned building plan the borrower is unable to commence
commercial operation. The loan account is in default.

Management replied (July 2013) that loan was disbursed in anticipation of the
unit getting the necessary completion certificate. WBFC failed to verify
availability of the requisite approval/ sanctions before releasing the loan.

. WBEFC had sanctioned (December 2009) a term loan of € 90 lakh to a
rice mill firm, which was secured by first charge on all fixed assets of the mill
including land. Mutation of land in favour of the borrower and conversion of
land for industrial usage were pre-disbursement terms. However, = 58.17 lakh
was disbursed (September 2011) to the borrower without fulfilment of either
of these conditions.

Subsequently, WBFC discovered (April 2012) that the borrower had
meanwhile sold the land while it was still mortgaged with WBFC. The loan
account remains in default.

3.7.3.7 Disbursement of loan without verifying pre-conditions

Once sanctioned, loans were disbursed after compliance with pre-
disbursement requirements such as ensuring promoter’s contribution to
project, physical inspection of the project by WBFC and mortgage/
hypothecation of collateral and primary security. In most cases, Audit
observed that average time taken to fulfil these pre-disbursement conditions
was two months. Audit also observed that WBFC, however, did not maintain
any register indicating details of compliance of each pre-disbursement
condition met by borrowers.

In audit it was seen that in eight” of 206 cases examined, loans were disbursed
within a day after sanction of loans. This indicated that the basic checks for
such loans, such as fulfilment of promoter’s obligation prior to loan
disbursement etc., were not applied. Audit obtained from Registrar of
Companies, annual financial statements of 15 corporate borrowers who had
defaulted in repayment of loans. It was noticed in respect of three of these
borrowers that information furnished by them to WBFC was not in agreement
with their financial statements as discussed at Paragraphs 3.7.3.8, 3.8.4 and
3.9.4.1. The deficiencies in test checked cases are discussed in the succeeding
paragraphs:

.8 Failure to ensure promoter’s contribution

. A company was sanctioned (3 December 2009) %75 lakh by the
WBEIC, Kolkata branch for expansion of its existing hotel at Bakkhali. The
entire loan of हैं 75 lakh was disbursed on the next day (4 December 2009).
Audit observed that terms of sanctioning the loan envisaged that the
shareholder’s equity was to be raised to हैं 22 lakh and unsecured loans raised
to हैं six lakh over and above the capital base of 31 March 2009 prior to release
of the loan. The Company submitted a certificate by a chartered
accountant (CA) vouchsafing fulfilment of these obligations.

Audit noticed that the company’s annual accounts for the years 2008-09 to
2010-11 filed with Registrar of Companies showed that it had neither brought
requisite equity nor the unsecured loan (Annexure 3.5). Further the veracity of
the certificate furnished also could not be vouchsafed in audit. The mandatory
requirement of liquid collateral security was also waived by WBFC without
any recorded reasons. The loan account is in default.

. Similarly, a hotel owned by promoters of above company, was
disbursed (September 2009/ March 2010) %twocrore loan based on a
chartered accountant’s (CA) certificate showing fulfilment of promoters’
obligation prior to the disbursement of loans. Veracity of the certificate could
not be vouchsafed in audit as the registration number and name of the CA did
not match with the data available on the Institute of Chartered Accountants of
India website. | Subsequent Tax Audit report of hotel
revealed (September 2010) that it had brought in only %5,305.92 as
promoter’s capital contribution to the project as against हैं 1.24 crore envisaged
in sanction approval. Moreover, the loan to the hotel was sanctioned by
waiving the mandatory requirement of liquid collateral security.

WBEFC failed to carry out due diligence of documents submitted by borrowers.
These projects remain incomplete and in default as of May 2013.

Management stated (July 2013) that out of eight loans (which were disbursed
on the next day after sanction), six were small loans where sanction and
disbursement activities were undertaken simultaneously. Remaining cases
were “their old and good clients”.

However, verification of pre-disbursement conditions cannot be overlooked
for either small loans or large loans. Moreover, since Management intimated
the pre-disbursement conditions to the borrower only in the sanction letter, it
follows that these pre-disbursement conditions were made known only on
sanction. Thus sanction and disbursement cannot be concurrent as
disbursement can only be made only after fulfilment of pre-disbursement
condition mentioned in the sanction letter.

Management accepted (October 2013) the observation and stated that due
diligence would be undertaken in future.

3.7.3.9 Exposure to defaulting sectors and credit concentration

Prudential norms require that a financial institution does not make bulk of its
investment in one type of industry or one business house.

49


Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

The lending policy (June 2012) of WBFC capped exposure to any given sector
at 10 per cent, except for Metal, Plastics and Textile industry for which WBFC
adopted a higher exposure of up to 25 per cent of outstanding advances.

However, Audit observed (Annexures 3.6 and 3.7) that amongst WBFC’s
investments in various sectors, wood, paper, rubber and transport sectors, had
high degree of non-performing assets with both the number of accounts as
well as principal outstanding each exceeding 50 percent rate of default.
Despite these trends, WBFC did not impose any restriction on lending to the
defaulting sectors. Test check revealed the following:

3.7.3.10 Failure to diversify risk

During October 2007 to July 2009 WBFC sanctioned eight term loans, six
additional term loans and six working capital loans aggregating ¥ 43.42 crore
to a group of seven companies for seven cold storages in Midnapore and
Bankura and one hotel at Burdwan. The loans were provided for renovation of
an existing cold storage, taking over and renovation of six closed cold storage
units and one unfinished hotel. Audit observed that in past five years there
was no increase in cold storage charges notified by the State Government. Yet
increasing revenues were justified in appraisal memoranda by projecting
reduction in costs and future increases in cold storage charges for each
subsequent loan. Test check further showed that the machineries were
purchased from a firm owned by principal promoter. Further, the loan
sanctioned to the hotel was ineligible in terms of lending policy as the
borrower’s grade was below the minimum requirements as discussed in
paragraph 3.7.1.2.

Group had defaulted in payment of interest and repayment of principal for all
these loans. Consequently, as of March 2013, recovery of all loans from the
group had become doubtful. The current outstanding position is given at
Annexure 3.4. Thus heavy exposure to a single group and same business
resulted in concentration of credit risk.

Management did not refute the above point but stated (July 2013) that
portfolio analysis had since been introduced in respect of bigger loan
proposals or proposals of the same nature regarding further credit exposure in
that sector.

3.73.11 Finance to ineligible borrowers

Section 25 of the Act provides that WBFC can only extend loan to industrial
concerns. Educational institutions are not industrial concerns defined under
the Act. Yet WBFC had extended five loans”® to four educational institutions
amounting to हैं 5.10 crore during the period under review. Without prior
reference to SIDBI, these five loans were approved by the Managing Director

6 TBA100 (₹ 0.40 crore), LCO40 (₹ 0.90 crore), LGE80 (® two crore), LINC2 (₹ 0.80 crore),
LINC6 @ one crore).

50


Chapter III Performance audit relating to Statutory Corporation

who was authorised to sanction loan of up to = two crore under delegated

authority.

Management replied (July 2013) that in some cases financial assistance was
allowed as the borrower was found eligible and support worthy. SIDBI had
also earlier given clearance for financing technical training institute.

The fact remains that SIDBI had turned down WBFC’s proposals to refinance
the above loans citing lack of provision in the Act. Financing of these entities
was against the provisions of the Act.

Above instances show that appraisal mechanism needs to be vastly improved
to ensure credibility and creditworthiness of the borrowers.

3. 8 Monitoring

WBEFC adopted (January 2008) SIDBI approved guidelines for follow up and
monitoring. The objectives of guidelines were to ensure utilisation of loans
for intended purposes, timely completion of projects without major deviations
and compliance to terms and conditions of sanctions while ensuring adequate
security, besides effective management of credit risks.

Above guidelines also required obtaining regular progress reports in respect of
project implementation, prescribed inspection of units, analysis of annual
accounts of loanee units including inter-firm comparisons in case of larger
units, discussion with unit’s bankers, etc.

Instances of non-adherence to these guidelines and deviations therefrom as
noticed by audit are discussed in the following paragraphs.

3.8.1 Insurance of primary security

WBFC requires insurance of all primary securities”’ at replacement value of
those assets. However, audit observed that WBFC only enforced insurance of
primary security against loan accounts which are not in default. Once an
account runs into default, the insurance of its assets was not renewed. WBFC
could not provide consistent data in this regard for the past five years in
respect of the value of its assets for which it maintained no records, though it
intimated that in 2012-13, out of 2,332 insurable units, only 488 units had
adequate insurance cover, while insurance in respect of 1,687 units was not
renewed during the last three years. Absence of insurance exposed WBFC to
financial risks because in case of loss of these assets, WBFC would not be able
to realise any claim from insurance.

Management agreed (July 2013) with the audit observations.

27 Mortgaged/ Hypothecated assets.

51


Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

3.8.2 Progress Reports

As per follow up and monitoring guideline, WBFC was required to obtain
Progress Report from borrowers, quarterly in case of projects under
implementation and half-yearly for projects under operation. Progress Reports
were to be compared with original project proposals on the basis of which
loans were sanctioned. However, WBFC did not obtain Progress Report from
borrowers once the project was put into operation. Thus it remained unaware
of proper implementation or progress of projects financed by it. As found
from records of WBFC, Siliguri branch, an illustrative instance of such non-
compliance is given below:

3.8.2.1 Poor monitoring of project implementation

A unit in Siliguri was sanctioned (September 2008) a term loan of € one crore
for developing a resort in Jalpaiguri with built up area of 11,348 sq ft. The
loan was disbursed between March 2009 and January 2010. In February 2010,
the borrower further sought हैं 60 lakh to finance cost over-run due to increase
in constructed area by 7,446 sqft. not envisaged earlier in the approved
project. Audit observed that the increase in capacity was effected without
even completing and commencing operation of the resort covering the area of
11,348 sq ft. as originally envisaged. However, instead of invoking
section 30 of the Act for violating terms of the loan, WBFC sanctioned
(August 2010) and disbursed (October 2010/ March 2011) %60 lakh as an
additional term loan. Even after sanction of term loan for the expansion, the
unit did not create required infrastructure for full fledged operation of the
resort. The account went into default in March 2011.

This indicated that progress of project was not known to WBFC in the absence
of regular and periodic progress reports. The fact that the borrower expanded
the resort without even constructing the infrastructure as initially envisaged
and finally could not create infrastructure needed for the resort, indicated poor
monitoring of the project by WBFC.

Management replied (July 2013) that while implementing the project, the unit
could not restrict the construction area as per approved scheme. The account
remains in default (October 2013).

Regarding absence of regular progress report, management replied (July 2013)
that post disbursement progress/ status report was sought on case to case basis.
However, audit had scrutinised 206 loan accounts and there was no case where
the Progress Report was either sought by WBFC or submitted by the borrower.
Management accepted the audit observations (October 2013).

3.8.3 Inspection of Assisted units

WBFC’s policy stipulates quarterly inspection of units during project
implementation stage and half yearly inspection at project operation stage.
Audit observed that post-disbursement inspections were not undertaken for all
units. Out of 2,421 units, WBFC intimated that only 779 units (32 per cent)

52


Chapter III Performance audit relating to Statutory Corporation

were inspected in 2012-13 and out of the remaining 1,642 units,
1,208 units (50 per cent) were not inspected for over two years.

In absence of regular inspection, WBFC could not assess the successful
completion of the project and its operational performance. Thus, it could not
take timely action to prevent default of the account.

3.8.3.1 Failure to identify assets

In course of the present review, Audit Team physically inspected 10 units,
financed by WBFC, along with their officers. Out of these only one unit had a
sign board indicating that its assets were mortgaged/ hypothecated to WBFC.
Display of mortgage sign enabled quick identification of mortgaged assets and
was a pre-condition of loan disbursement.

In one unit, it was noticed that the assets were mixed with other assets of
another sister entity under the same group. WBFC, despite having inspected
the same unit earlier, failed to identify the assets that were financed by it. In
respect of another unit in the business of food products, it was noticed that the
premises were not clearly demarked and it shared facilities with another unit in
similar line of business.

In reply management stated (July 2013) that post disbursement inspection was
carried out as and when necessary, on case to case basis and depending on
situation. It was, however, observed by audit that WBFC carried out post
disbursement inspections only in respect of defaulting units.

Management accepted (October 2013) the observation.
3.8.4 Failure to inspect books of accounts

WBFC’s monitoring guideline and terms of sanction of loan require that the
borrower submit its Annual Accounts regularly and promptly to WBFC.
However, WBFC did not obtain or check all Annual Accounts of borrowers
after disbursement of loans. An instance is given below:

A company was disbursed (March 2009 and April 2010) ₹ 82 lakh on the basis
of Annual Accounts for 2007-08 and subsequent certificates of the CA
vouchsafing that the borrower had met the pre-disbursement conditions of
raising equity capital of € 62 lakh and unsecured loan of रैं 20 lakh. Audit
observed from annual financial statements filed with Registrar of Companies
that the borrower had raised equity capital of = 9.40 lakh only (Annexure 3.8).
WBFC failed to check the veracity of the certificates or claims made by the
borrower. The unit was inspected twice in April 2010 and February 2012 but
the books of accounts were not inspected by WBFC. It was seen that the first
inspection report indicated procurement of 38 items of plant and machinery,
but the second inspection report mentioned that these items could not be
found.

Management accepted (July 2013) the audit observations.

53


Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

3.8.5 Appointment of nominee director

As per terms of sanction of loan, WBFC was entitled to nominate directors on
the Board of financed units and appoint verification auditors. Borrower was
required to insure mortgaged assets and submit financial statements to WBFC.
Failure of these conditions attracted two per cent penal interest. However,
WBEFC did not enforce these clauses during 2008-09 to 2012-13.

WBEC did not appoint nominee directors on the Board of the assisted units.
Management replied (October 2013) that nominee directors faced legal
harassment, hence they were not appointed. However, WBFC’s terms of
sanction of loan provide for such appointment.

3.9 Recovery of Loans
3.9.1 Recovery Manual

WBFC had adopted (February 2008) a Recovery Manual to implement
uniform recovery procedure. The Manual specified norms for asset
classification and recovery procedure for each class of asset. It also laid down
norms for revision of repayment terms for borrowers, creation of second
charge on primary security, prepayment of loans by borrowers, compromise
settlement with borrowers including one-time settlements, enforcement of
recovery provisions of the Act enabling takeover of assets of the defaulters,
invoking personal guarantees, issue of ‘no due certificate’ and release of
security documents etc.

Non-implementation of procedures mentioned in Recovery Manual is one of
the main reasons for increase in non-performing assets of WBFC.
Irregularities observed during 2008-09 to 2012-13 are discussed in following
paragraphs.

3.9.2 Classification of Assets

Loan accounts are classified according to the asset classification guidelines of
SIDBI. Loans are classified as per extant guidelines, depending upon the
payments made by the loanees.

Details of loan assets and their classification can be seen in Table 3.5.
Standard assets are assets held by non-defaulting accounts. The
non-performing assets included assets classified as sub-standard, doubtful and
loss assets.”*

28 Sub-standard assets are those loan accounts which are in default for three to 15 months.
Doubtfiul assets are the loans in default for more than 15 months. Loss assets are loans that do
not have primary security back-up.

It is seen that WBFC could reduce NPA only marginally from 26.58 per cent
in 2008-09 to 26.03 per cent in 2012-13. As per the MoU with SIDBI (June
2009), WBFC was required to reduce its NPA to 10 per cent within five years.
Management attributed (July 2013) the “higher than standard NPA” to
stringent norms of income recognition and mentioned that efforts were on to
contain the sub-standard assets within the norms.

3.9.3 Overdue analysis

Analysis of amount of overdue principal, overdue interest, amount due for
recovery and amount recovered during the period from 2008-09 to 2012-13 as
depicted at Annexure 3.9 revealed the following facts:

Target for recovery was fixed on an ad-hoc basis. It was seen that targets
fixed during the last five years varied from 49 per cent to 65 per cent for
recoverable dues in respect of principal amounts, while in respect of interest,
recovery was from nine per cent to 15 per cent. Low targets were kept only to
inflate their performance artificially, since as per terms of its MoU with
SIDBI, the actual recovery (principal and interest) for standard assets should
not be less than 90 per cent of the targets, excluding pre-payments. Actual
rate of recovery of the principal by WBFC varied between 51 per cent and
60 per cent while recovery of interest varied only between eight per cent to
15 per cent of the total dues.

In its reply, Management stated (October 2013) that recovery target was fixed
on category of loans and not over the entire loan portfolio. Reply was silent
about fixation of target at a low level.

3.9.4 Rescheduling of Loans

As per the recovery manual of WBFC, revision of repayment terms under
standard or sub-standard accounts are allowed in deserving cases which are
sustainable and viable. Table 3.6 shows details of loans rescheduled during
2009-13”.

Audit noticed that out of the 98 instances of rescheduling mentioned above,
loans of eight borrowers aggregating हैं 21.33 crore were restructured twice.
Twenty two of these borrowers remained defaulters for periods ranging
between three months to 27 months even after rescheduling of their loans with
% 27.66 crore” becoming recoverable from them as of March 2013. Audit
observed that the rescheduling of loan accounts was done by WBFC without
any systematic reassessment of the viability of the project, an illustrative
instance of which is given below:

3.9.4.1 Rephasement of loan without assessment

A company was sanctioned (January 2010) a term loan of %3.15 crore by
Head Office of WBFC to manufacture HDPE/ PVC pipes at Jalpaiguri.
Between March 2010 and August 2010, WBFC had disbursed % 1.92 crore to
the unit. By July 2011, the unit was in default and sought additional loan as
well as rescheduling of the existing loans. WBFC did not analyse either the
viability of the project at that stage, or the cause for delay in completion of the
project. In September 2011, WBFC rescheduled the loan and disbursed
additional € 42.15 lakh out of which हैं 6.74 lakh was adjusted with outstanding
interest and thereby upgrading the default account to a standard account.

Audit observed that the project remained incomplete due to Promoter’s failure
to contribute % 1.52 crore towards share capital and % 50.01 lakh towards
unsecured loan (Annexure 3.10). The Promoter’s contribution was a pre-
requisite for disbursement of loan from WBFC. The account remains in
default.

Further, restructured accounts require additional five per cent provisioning in
the books of WBFC, which was also not done.

No specific reply was given by Management in this case which merely stated
(July 2013) that sometime in order to hasten implementation of the project and
after allowing a second charge on the fixed assets in favour of the banker of
the unit, loans were rescheduled. No data, however, could be found in respect
of second charges.

Chapter III Performance audit relating to Statutory Corporation

3.9.5 Recovery of dues by invoking SFC Act

WBIC is empowered by the Act to enforce immediate repayment of loan for
violation of terms of sanction (Section 30), taking over of the assets and
selling them to recover its dues (Section 29) and approach the District
Judge for enforcement of its rights (Section 31). Besides these, WBFC can
also invoke personal guarantees, encash liquid collateral securities and adjust
that against its dues.

Instances of departure from these provisions under the Act as observed by
audit are given below:

3.9.5.1 Failure to invoke Sections 29 and 30 of SFC Act

. WBEC had disbursed (May and September 2009) a loan of % 12 lakh
to a firm against hypothecation of its assets. In October 2010, WBFC learnt
that the borrower had sold off an asset worth % six lakh for हैं 2.50 lakh and
appropriated the proceeds. However, only in March 2011 WBFC invoked the
provisions of Sections 29 and 30 and took over the business. Further, instead
of disposing the business through open sale, WBFC handed it over to another
entrepreneur recommended by the borrower. Even after the handover, the loan
account remained in default.

. In August 2011, Punjab National Bank (PNB) had informed WBFC
that a Company had mortgaged the same piece of land measuring 4.43 acres at
Mejia under Bankura district to both PNB and WBFC to obtain term loan of
% five crore from WBFC. WBFC on further scrutiny found (June 2012) that
the title deed submitted by the borrower was fake. However no action was
taken by WBFC to invoke provisions of section 30 of the Act which allows
recall of the loan in case of submission of misleading information by the
borrower. The loan account remained in default.

In the course of performance audit, 12 cases"! were noticed (May to
June 2013) by audit where the borrowers had submitted incorrect/false
Chartered Accountant’s certificate to state that they had brought in their share
of promoter’s contribution. In none of the cases, had WBFC invoked
Section 30 (July 2013) on this ground nor initiated any legal action for
furnishing false certificates.

Although WBFC was empowered by the Act to take quick action for recovery
of default loans by taking physical possession of the assets, audit observed that
there were 41 loan accounts in default against which Management had not
initiated any action under the Act.

अ LTDO 07, LHO 81 & 83, LMTAN044, 45 & 42, LAN 58, 59, 61 & 62, LBR 35, 38 & 39,
LMTSR 224, 225, 227 & 228, LMTOH 010 & 011, LMTJA 031 & 32, LMTAN 050, LDR
09, LSTSR 01, LDI 28.

57


Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

In its reply, Management stated (October 2013) that if it invoked Section 29
too early in respect of a defaulting unit, there would be no further scope for
negotiation and repayment of the loans by the borrower. The loan account
would remain in default. But action as provided in the Act should have been
taken.

3.10 Internal Control
3.10.1 Absence of allocation of duties

WBEIC did not prepare allocation of duties and responsibilities. Consequently,
the duties and responsibilities of employees or consultants were not classified,
in the absence of which it would be difficult to fix responsibility.

3.10.2 Failure to comply with norms

Audit also observed that WBFC failed to adhere to RBI’s “Know Your
Customer” (KYC) and anti-money laundering (AML) norms to some extent.
The KYC was not updated annually. Also WBFC did not examine the sources
of interest-free unsecured loans brought by the promoters.

3.10.3 Lack of internal check in WBFC’s accounting system

Audit scrutiny” of vouchers passed during 2008-09 to 2012-13 in the
computerised accounting system of WBFC showed that out of
51,222 vouchers, 718* vouchers (1.4 per cent) were prepared and approved by
the same individual indicating violation of principle of segregation of duties.
Audit also observed that in 237 vouchers (including 123 vouchers pertaining
to instalments of principal and interest payments on loan vouchers) during
2008-09 to 2012-13, the dates of approval of the vouchers preceded their
corresponding dates of preparation. WBFC’s computerised accounting system
accepted such entries which indicated lack of validation control in the system.
In the absence of such controls, accounting data manipulation and attendant
risks cannot be ruled out.

Management agreed (July 2013) with the audit observations and noted it for
future guidance.

3.10.4 Failure to form Audit Committee

Till December 2011, WBFC did not have any audit committee which was then
constituted with three independent directors. Since then it had met only twice.

* Using IDEA.
3 A goregating to € 630.82 crore.

58


Chapter III Performance audit relating to Statutory Corporation

WBFC did not have an internal audit charter and the audit committee did not
review the internal audit reports. Also, it did not approve the internal audit
programmes for the year. The internal audit department was headed by a
General Manager who also held charge of Accounts and Operations. Thus it
did not work independently.

Conclusion

1. WBFC was not a significant source of financing MiSME units in the
State.

2. WBFC’s appraisal mechanism was inadequate. It did not seem to
encourage first generation entrepreneurs.

3. | WBEC did not follow its own monitoring and follow-up guideline.
4. WBFC’s internal control processes were weak.
Recommendations:

I. Government may review the objectives and viability of operations of
WBFC.

2. WBFC should strengthen its appraisal mechanism.

3. It should ensure greater compliance with its monitoring and follow-up
guidelines.

Chapter IV

4 Compliance Audit

Important audit findings arising out of test check of transactions made by the
State Government companies/ corporations are included in this chapter.

Government Companies

THE WEST BENGAL POWER DEVELOPMENT CORPORATION
LIMITED AND THE DURGAPUR PROJECTS LIMITED

4.1 Undue benefit to a private- sector joint venture partner

The West Bengal Power Development Corporation Limited selected a
private-sector partner, to set up a joint venture (JV) company for
operation of captive coal mines, without open tender. Moreover, there
were contractual drawbacks in terms and conditions.

The West Bengal Power Development Corporation Limited (WBPDCL) was
set up (5 July 1985) with primary objective of electricity generation. Ministry
of Coal (MoC) had allotted seven! coal blocks to West Bengal State Electricity
Board (WBSEB)/ WBPDCL between July 1995 and February 2009, out of
which one” block was surrendered (October 2011) by WBPDCL as it was not
found suitable for power generation.

Audit reviewed records of Department of Power and Non Conventional
Energy Sources, Government of West Bengal (GoWB) and WBPDCL
covering the period from April 2003 to March 2012 to assess whether -

e selection of joint venture partner for development and operation of
allotted blocks was done following due processes;

© mines were operationalised within the stipulated time schedule; and

© contractual terms and conditions for extraction of coal were properly
drawn up to safeguard interests of WBPDCL.

Audit findings are discussed in the subsequent paragraphs.
4.1.1 Selection of joint venture (JV) partner on nomination basis

WBSEB in April 1995 selected an Agency, in the private sector, as JV partner
on nomination basis and entered into an agreement for operation of captive

1 Tara (East) to WBSEB and Tara (West), Barjore, Gangaramchak, Gangaramchak -
Bhadulia, Pachwara (North) and Kalyaneswari to WBPDCL.
? Kalyaneswari coal block.

61


Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

coal mines allotted to WBSEB. In January 1996, WBPDCL also joined the
agreement to set up a JV company for mining of coal from captive coal blocks
at Tara(East) and Tara(West) allotted to WBSEB and WBPDCL.
Accordingly, in February 1996, a JV company viz. Bengal EMTA Coalmines
Limited (BECML) was incorporated with WBSEB, WBPDCL and the private
agency holding 14 per cent, 12 per cent and 74 per cent of the paid up capital
respectively. BECML, as per the agreement, entrusted the private agency with
the sole responsibility of operation, control, management and safety aspect of
captive mines. Agreement also provided that WBSEB/ WBPDCL would
obtain requisite leases of the mines from Gol/ GoWB and concerned
authorities.

In April 2002, the agreement was revised and The Durgapur Projects Limited
(DPL- a State PSU) became a shareholder in BECML instead of WBSEB.
BECML was informed in July 2003 about the State Government’s decision to
let BECML operate subsequent coal blocks as and when allotted to WBPDCL.
Consequently, BECML continued to hold the four subsequently allotted
blocks* with the private agency operating, controlling and managing all coal
blocks of WBPDCL.

No proposals containing evaluation of commercial expediency, techno
economic parameters, quantification of likely benefits and risk factors, if any,
for selection of private agency as JV partner and continuation of BECML for
operation of coal blocks through agency were presented to WBPDCL’s Board
of Directors. Consequently, justification for proposal was not on record.

Guidelines issued by the Ministry of Finance mention that a JV, wherein
more than 50 per cent shareholding is held by a private partner, must be
treated at par with other private companies and any procurement of goods and
services from such a JV must follow the normal tendering process as per GFR.

As per West Bengal Financial Rules (WBFR), open tender should invariably
be invited for execution of works worth हैं 20,000 or more. The Central
Vigilance Commission (CVC) also stated that tendering process is a basic
requirement for the award of contract by any government agency etc.

Government accepted (August 2013) that BECML was appointed on
nomination basis. It further stated that arrangement was favourable to
WBPDCL/ DPL as it provided for invoicing of coal based on (i) actual
quantity received and (ii) analysis of grade of coal at recipient’s end.

The reply does not address awarding of works to a private sector company by

a government agency without open tender. As regards terms being favourable,
the same could also have been achieved through open tender.

3 Barjore, Gangaramchak, Gangaramchak-Bhadulia and Pachwara (North).

62


Chapter IV Compliance Audit Observations

4.1.2 Delay in commencement of mining

MoC’s guidelines provide that coal production should commence within
36 months (42 months in case forest land is involved) from the date of
allocation in respect of open cast mines. Audit observed that in respect of all
four blocks allotted between April 2003 and March 2012, delay in
commencement of production ranged between 26 to 80 months as detailed at
Annexure 4.1. Some significant delays are discussed below.

4.1.2.1 Barjore block, allotted by MoC on 23 June 2003, was expected to
commence production from 23 December 2006. The mine was ready to
commence production only in March 2009 after a delay of 26 months out of
which 13 months delay was subsequent to receipt of the Coal Controller’s
permission (February 2008) to open the mine. Extraction of coal, however,
could not commence till June 2009 due to evacuation constraints arising from
lack of follow-up with railways for permission to load coal from BECML’s
Hazrathpur siding. Further in June 2012 production had to be stopped as
adjacent land required for mining and allied activities was not available to
BECML as yet. It was seen in audit that 17.64 ha of vested land applied for in
March and May 2005 had not been transferred by GoWB as it exceeded the
land ceiling and required special approval for the same. Finally, BECML, in
August 2013 applied for requisite approvals to hold land in excess of statutory
ceiling to the Department of Land and Land Reforms.

Government responded (August 2013) that supply from this coal block had
commenced in 2010-11 but offered no explanation for intervening period of
delay from February 2008 (receipt of requisite permission from Coal
Controller) to actual commencement in 2010-11. Besides, reason for
BECML/’s belated application for holding of land in excess of ceiling was also
not clarified by WBPDCL.

4.1.2.2 Gangaramchack and Gangaramchack-Bhadulia blocks (IGGB)
allotted on 23 June 2003, were being developed in an integrated manner.
Production was yet to commence (August 2013) against scheduled date of
23 December 2006. Delay of 80 months was mainly attributable to non-receipt
of approval for forest clearance, sought in November 2005. Delay in forest
clearance was due to the fact that BECML had identified land for compensatory
afforestation, which as per Forest department, GoWB was ‘protected forest
land’. Consequently, Forest department had not forwarded the proposal to the
Ministry of Environments and Forests, Gol till September 2012.

Government stated (August 2013) that in May 2013, Regional Conservator of
Forests (Central), Gol had intimated that all new proposals for forest clearance
in West Bengal had been put on hold till compensatory afforestation (CA)
lands procured in lieu of forest land for earlier approved projects were notified
as Reserve Forest/ Protected Forest. Government further added that one lakh

63


Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

tonnes of coal had already been exposed‘ but evacuation was not possible
because of inaccessibility from the mine to railway siding due to a canal of
Irrigation department. It was anticipated that Irrigation department would
accord ‘No Objection’ Certificate (NOC) by end of August 2013 to construct,
within four months from date of issue of order, a bridge on the canal as well as
strengthen and widen the causeway. Coal was expected to be dispatched
within one month of construction.

The reply indicated that delay in production was not only due to non receipt of
forest clearance but also to non availability of requisite infrastructure. Delay
occurred due to identifying unsuitable land for compulsory afforestation and
failure to create requisite infrastructure to evacuate coal by BECML.

4.1.2.3 Panchwara (North) block was allotted on 26 April 2005 and
scheduled to come into production from 28 May 2011. Though pre-mining
activities were completed, production could not commence even 27 months
beyond scheduled date due to delayed transfer of land (August 2013).
Although application for land was submitted in August 2008 i.e. one year
ahead of schedule, the land was transferred to BECML only in June 2013.
Besides, due to non-receipt of Stage-II forest clearance, the Jharkhand
Pollution Control Board had not issued necessary “Consent to operate” for the
mine (August 2013).

Government added (August 2013) that delays in commencement of production
from IGGB and Panchwara (North) coal blocks were in some cases beyond
control and unavoidable.

Delay in production of coal from its captive mines compelled WBPDCL to
purchase/ import 26.53 lakh tonnes of coal during three years from 2009-10 to
2011-12 at an average price ranging between % 4,314 and & 4,781 per tonne to
meet its coal requirements resulting in avoidable expenditure amounting to
₹ 435.51 crore.

Thus failure to plan properly and liaise with various authorities resulted in
stoppage of production from captive mines thereby compelling WBPDCL to
purchase/ import coal at an additional cost.

The JV agreement (January 1996/ April 2002) between three partners till 2001
and then DPL as replacement for one partner reflected the following
infirmities in terms and conditions :

Chapter IV Compliance Audit Observations

4.1.3.1 Linking sale price of coal extracted from captive mines with Coal
India Limited (CIL) sale price

Captive mines ensure that coal received would be at the least cost. Coal
requirements of power sector should be supplied at prices determined strictly
on a cost-to-produce basis subject to benchmark efficiency norms as per The
Expert Committee on Road Map for Coal Sector Reforms’. The terms of JV,
however, stipulated that in respect of Tara (East and West) blocks, coal would
be transferred to WBPDCL/ DPL at a price of 19.5 per cent below the
prevailing base price of CIL for respective grades on the date of supply. For
subsequent blocks, the discount was enhanced to 21 per cent with the statutory
levies’ to be borne by WBPDCL.

Audit observed that BECML in turn awarded extraction contract to the private
agency. The extraction contract price was linked to CIL price instead of actual
cost of extraction, which had increased the coal cost for WBPDCL.

Government stated (August 2013) that in respect of the Tara blocks, as
concept was new, the agreement provided for this clause. It added that in
respect of subsequent blocks, the coal purchase agreement would be modified
to determine coal prices on ‘cost plus’ basis.

Linking sale price/ extraction price with CIL would burden WBPDCL/ DPL,
the owners and benefit the private agency in case of increase in CIL coal
prices.

In 2008-09 the mining cost® of coal was € 427.82 per tonne in open cast mines
against which the average transfer price to BECML was % 584 per tonne and
average sale price to WBPDCL was % 725.57 per tonne. Audit observed that
during 2008-09, WBPDCL purchased coal from its captive mine at हैं 725.57
per tonne as against the extraction cost of ¥ 427.82 per tonne incurred by
agency as illustrated in the following flowchart.

4.1.3.2 Avoidable expenditure towards sales tax/ value added tax

The JV agreement stipulates that WBPDCL and DPL would buy coal and all
applicable taxes and duties including sales tax would be payable at actuals by
WBPDCL and DPL. The said clause in the JV agreement was deficient as the
nature of transaction involved was not that of sales but of providing services
by way of extraction and supply of coal by BECML to the mine owners viz.
WBPDCL and DPL. This was also confirmed by clarification issued by MoC
in September 2008 stating that transfer of coal from a JV/SPV mining
company to the block allocatees cannot be treated as sale of coal from the
mining company to the end use company. No amendment was, however,
made in the agreement and WBPDCL continued to pay sales tax on the coal

2 On 41,32,101 tonnes (WBPDCL : 34,05,236 tonnes, DPL : 7,26,865 tonnes) at the rate of
₹ 156.18 per tonne.

10 Seventy four per cent of हैं 58.50 crore on 41,32,101 tonnes to WBPDCL and DPL at the
rate of € 141.57 per tonne.

Chapter IV Compliance Audit Observations

supplied by BECML. It was noticed in audit that during the period between
2009-10 and 2011-12, WBPDCL had paid an amount of & 52.78 crore towards
sales tax liability.

Government while not refuting the point of applicability of service tax, stated
(August 2013) that if the supplies of coal from captive coal blocks were not
considered as sale, the same would attract service tax, rates of which were
higher than the rate of sales tax. However, audit point is on non-compliance
with provisions of Acts.

4.1.3.3 Grade slippage" claims forgone

As per clause 36 of JV agreement, quality would be tested and assessed as per
joint sampling at the power station end. Further as per the accounting policy
of BECML in 2008-09 to 2010-11, invoice would be raised on the basis of ‘C’
grade coal and in case of deviation of quality found later, revenue would be
adjusted on the basis of claims raised by the power plants. Audit observed
that for the 2009-10 and 2010-11, WBPDCL raised grade slippage claims of
% 230.97 crore which, however, was settled at % 152.45 crore by BECML,
thereby forgoing claims amounting to % 78.52 crore. Reasons for the same,
though called for, were not furnished to audit.

For the year 2011-12, Government intimated that against grade slippage
claims of % 57.62 crore raised by WBPDCL, BECML had settled the claim at
% 55.12 crore on the basis of joint sampling as per agreement. As WBPDCL
was raising the grade slippage claims based on the joint sampling, settlement
of claims for lower amount needed clarification.

4.1.3.4 Creation of assets in the name of BECML

As per clause 20 of the JV agreement, WBSEB and WBPDCL will obtain
necessary lease of the mines from Gol/ GoWB and concerned authorities and
the mines will be operated by BECML. The agreement also provided that
WBSEB/ WBPDCL would render assistance in respect of acquisition of
private land and allotment of vested lands by GoWB required for mining
operation. WBPDCL in August 2008 sought a legal opinion on acquisition of
land for the project. As per the legal opinion it was a better option to acquire
immovable assets like land in the name of WBPDCL as acquiring it in the
name of the BECML could lead to dichotomy as mining rights rest with
WBPDCL. It also mentioned that in case of termination of the arrangement it
would facilitate allocation of mine and its operation to another operator if the
assets are acquired in the name of WBPDCL. However WBPDCL allowed
BECML to continue to acquire/ lease assets like land, mines and railway
sidings in the name of the JV company.

|! Difference in the base price of declared grade and analysed grade of coal.

67


Audit Report (PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2013

Government responded (August 2013) that so far, WBPDCL had not made
any investment for acquisition of any asset. Cost of all the assets have been
invested by BECML and accordingly held in its name in the books of the Joint
Venture Company.

By allowing BECML to acquire immovable assets in its name, WBPDCL has
put itself in a disadvantageous position which may restrict it from the option
of selecting another operator through competitive bidding, as it would have to
depend on BECML, for utilising these fixed assets.

Conclusion

WBPDCL selected a JV partner on nomination basis without open tendering.
In addition, WBPDCL also continued to operate subsequent coal blocks
through BECML. Benefit of having its own captive mines was passed on to
JV partner. WBPDCL had incurred additional expenditure due to delays in
extraction of coal, stoppage of mining operations, payment of sales tax/ VAT
and non-recovery of grade slippage as per the terms of the contract.

In addition, WBPDCL had compromised its interest by acquiring the fixed
assets like land, railway siding etc. in the name of BECML.

THE WEST BENGAL POWER DEVELOPMENT CORPORATION
LIMITED
4.2 Avoidable expenditure on acquisition of an unsuitable coal block

The West Bengal Power Development Corporation Limited incurred
avoidable expenditure of % 6.68 crore besides blocking up of capital

amounting to 12.90 crore in acquiring a coal block which was
subsequently surrendered due to its unsuitability.

The West Bengal Power Development Corporation Limited (WBPDCL), a
wholly owned Government company operates five'® thermal power stations
for which the principal fuel is non-coking coal. WBPDCL applied
(May 2005) for Kalyaneswari (East of Damagoria) coal block within the
command areas of Bharat Coking Coal Limited (BCCL), to partly meet the
coal requirement for capacity addition of 3,000 MW. Coal India Limited
(CIL) in February 2006 had informed Government of West Bengal (GoWB)
that there was a possibility of the block turning to be of coking variety which
could be confirmed only from the final Geological Report (GR), expected to
be ready by June 2006. CIL also added that it would not be possible for CIL to
recommend allocation of that block to WBPDCL if the final GR confirmed
that the reserves were primarily of coking coal which was not suitable for

"Thermal power stations at Kolaghat, Bandel, Bakreshwar, Santaldih and Sagardighi.

68


Chapter IV Compliance Audit Observations

power generation and mentioned that the block would involve a lot of
rehabilitation efforts because of likely dislocation of three villages and
diversion of GT Road bye pass, power lines etc.

WBPDCL, however without ascertaining the grade of coal, continued to
pursue with Ministry of Coal (MoC) for allotment of the coal block and also
agreed (August 20077) to transfer all grades of coking coal from this block to
BCCL at mutually agreed transfer price which would be reviewed after five
years. Finally, MoC allotted (February 2009) the block to WBPDCL with the
condition that all coking coal obtained from the block would be handed over
to BCCL at discounts'’* to the notified prices prevailing as on
8 November 2008. WBPDCL submitted (May 2010) bank guarantee’> for
= 114.38 crore towards annual royalty to MoC. Thereafter it obtained a copy
of the Geological Report prepared by Central Mine Planning & Design
Institute Limited (CMPDIL) in June 2006 which confirmed that all proven
coal reserves at Kalyaneswari were of coking grade.

Meanwhile WBPDCL, at its end, appointed a consultant in April 2010 to
prepare pre-feasibility report of mining and other allied works'’. The
Consultant recommended (July 2010) handing over the block to BCCL on
account of the fact that (a) GR report had confirmed that all the proven coal
reserves were of coking grade and (b) an estimated expenditure of
1,263.91 crore would have to be incurred towards pre-development
clearances including resettlement, rehabilitation, land acquisition etc.

WBPDCL, in January 2011, decided to proceed for mining of the block on the
presumption that the transfer price!” of coking coal would be reviewed in
November 2014 thereby enhancing the viability of the proposal to sell coal to
BCCL and released रैं 12.90 crore to BCCL for supplying geological data and
exploration report for the block.

Finally, in September 2011, WBPDCL, on the direction of GoWB, decided to
return the block. MoC cancelled the allocation in October 2011. WBPDCL
had, by then, incurred aggregate expenditure of हैं 19.58 crore towards
Geological Report (% 12.90 crore) and bank guarantee charges (% 6.68 crore).

Thus, WBPDCL’s pursuit for an unsuitable coal block, overlooking the
Consultant’s advice and the opinion of CIL, led to avoidable loss of
हैं 6.68 crore as bank charges paid against bank guarantee and blocking up of
investment of € 12.90 crore on the Geological Report.

4.3 Avoidable payment of interest due to short deposit of advance
income tax

advance tax and self assessment tax arising out of incorrect estimation of
taxable income.

According to provisions of section 208 read with section 211 of Income Tax
Act, 1961 (Act), every company is required to pay for each financial year,
quarterly instalments of advance tax within their due dates at rates'* prescribed
in the Act, if the amount of income tax payable during the financial year is or
exceeds & 10,000. If instalments of advance tax deposited were less than the
prescribed percentages, the assessee company is liable to pay interest under
provisions of section 23487 and 234C” of the Act. This interest is to be
calculated and deposited with the balance tax determined on self assessment.

It was incumbent on assessee companies to fairly assess taxable income to
avoid the incidence of interest. It is standard practice of all business entities to
compute advance tax payable in each quarter and every company is required to
prepare quarterly accounts to determine taxable income and actual tax thereon.

Audit examined the system of estimating incomes of those PSUs that had
earned profits in the financial years 2009-2012 with regard to tax deposited on

38 15 per cent, 45 per cent, 75 per cent and 100 per cent of assessed tax by 15 June, 15 September,
15 December and 15 March respectively.

3१ Tf advance tax paid was less than 90 per cent of the assessed tax, interest was payable at the rate
of one per cent per month or part thereof on the amount by which the advance tax paid falls short
of assessed tax.

20 Interest at the rate of one per cent per month or part thereof on the amount short deposited
against cumulative instalments of advance tax for the period of three months.

70


Chapter IV Compliance Audit Observations

income within the due dates, income tax returns, challans, assessment orders,
demand notices etc. It was noticed that seven PSUs”! had either overestimated
or underestimated their tax liability in one or more years. Three PSUs
mentioned in Table 4.1 below underestimated their income for the year
2011-12 leading to short deposit of advance tax and self assessment tax as a
result of which they had to pay avoidable interest under the provisions of the
Act ibid.

the printing industry for long and should have been in a position to estimate
income to a reasonable degree of certainty.

total collections by its banker between April 2011 and March 2013 had

in a timely manner™*. The agreement was renewed” from time to time.

As per the agreement between the two companies signed in April 2010, the

e The provision of daily transfer of cash was not followed during the period.

During 10 months, funds were transferred only once or twice. In one month
(June 2012), there was not even a single transfer. In one instance, it took as
many as 49 days to transfer funds (19 May to 7 July 2012).

e Even on those occasions when remittances were made, the entire balance
was never transferred. Instead, on 161 days out of 192, the bank had remitted
between 0.53 to 89.80°° per cent only of the available balance, while in the
remaining 31 instances, 90.027’ per cent or more was remitted. Consequently,
amounts ranging from हैं 0.06 lakh (1 May/ 2 May 2010) to % 34.85 crore
(29 August 2012) remained idle in the current account with the remitting bank,
clearly indicating absence of a system of monitoring and reconciliation
between the two PSUs regarding remittance of collections.

e Meanwhile WBSEDCL was continuously facing large cash deficits and
financial crisis, leading to enhancement of its overall cash credit limits/ short
term loans from हैं 300 crore to % 2,500 crore with their different bankers in
several stages”*. To meet the working capital requirements, it obtained,
between 2010-11 and 2012-13, cash credit facilities from eight” scheduled
commercial banks including same branch of UBI to whom they paid interest
on their cash credit balances at the rate of 12.25 and 11.90 per cent during
2011-12 and 2012-13 respectively.

Delays in transferring the funds by UBI, Rajarhat was one reason for the
enhanced borrowings by WBSEDCL to fulfil their working capital
requirements to the extent of funds lying idle in non-interest bearing current
account with the bank, which otherwise would have saved them interest®’ of
र₹ 1.83 crore on avoidable cash credit availed between April 2011 and March
2013, besides mitigating the problems they were facing in respect of liquidity.

4.5 Additional expenditure on bill collection charges

West Bengal State Electri Distribution Company Limited determined
service charges for collection of bills at a higher rate on account of
calculation on incorrect data as well as allowing variable components at
higher rates, leading to excess expenditure of हैं 1.25 crore between
November 2011 and March 2013.

West Bengal State Electricity Distribution Company Limited (WBSEDCL)
entered (February 2008) into a tripartite agreement with West Bengal State
Rural Development Agency (WBSRDA) and a private agency and awarded
(June 2008) to the private agency work of collection and transfer to
WBSEDCL-payments against electricity bills from consumers at a service
charge of ® five per transaction (money receipt issued). The payments were to
be collected through service centres operated by village level
entrepreneurs’ (VLEs). The agreement was valid from July 2008 to
June 2010 and was extended (October 2010) till June 2011. The agreement
provided for review of the rate of service charge in July 2009. A fresh
agreement was signed in July 2011, which was valid up to June 2013.

WBSEDCL did not review the service charge in July 2009 as provided in the
agreement. Instead the private agency sought (October 2010/ March 2011)
enhancement of service charge from % five to nine per transaction with
breakup of cost components. Although the then Chairman and Managing
Director of WBSEDCL had approved (February 2011) % 6.52 per transaction,

cannot be taken and that considering the inflationary effect of the economy,
upward revision of rates was considered reasonable.

agreement of February 2008 envisaged rollout of services at 4,937 service
centres, indicating that concept of authorised VLEs was an afterthought.
Besides, it is pertinent to add that WBSEDCL was also collecting bills through
West Bengal Postal Circle and collection charges of five per bill remained
constant from April 2008 to date (July 2013).

4.6 Loss due to defective project appraisal and acceptance of flawed
security

West Bengal Industrial Development Corporation Limited incurred loss

of € 95 lakh due to defective project appraisal and release of short term
loan against flawed security.

West Bengal Industrial Development Corporation Limited (WBIDC)
sanctioned (May 2005) and released (July 2005/ March 2006) equity
assistance of € 45 lakh to a private sector company (the company) for setting
up an export oriented pineapple processing project at Siliguri, Darjeeling
district, the largest pineapple growing area in India. In terms of the share
subscription agreement (June 2005) with it, on completion of three years from
the date of commencement of commercial production, investee company’s
Principal Promoter and holding company would jointly buy back the entire
shares of the company held by WBIDC. The private sector company also

34

‘or 74.09 lakh bills collected between November 2011 and March 2013 on the additional
rate of & 1.69 per transaction (® 7.52 minus हैं 5.83).

76


Chapter IV Compliance Audit Observations

availed (2005-06 and 2006-07) loans from the banks against hypothecation of
fixed assets, land and building and entire current assets.

Audit observed that pineapple cultivation is seasonal* in West Bengal with
harvesting from June to November and arrivals peaking in July and August.
The Company did not consider the need to ensure continuous supply of
pineapples of requisite size and quality throughout the year and WBIDC,
while appraising the feasibility of the project overlooked the need for the
Company to tie up with pineapple farmers/ traders to ensure continued supply.

The Company faced shortage of pineapples of requisite quality and size and
could not run the unit for more than three months in a year i.e. August to
October since commissioning in June 2006. Consequently, its working capital
facilities with the banks became irregular, repayment of loans was re-scheduled
(July 2009) and it was unable to obtain further working capital from the banks.
As a final chance for revival of the unit by targeting a larger share of the
domestic market, it then approached (July 2009) WBIDC for a short term loan
of € 50 lakh to purchase raw materials.

WBIDC sanctioned (August 2009) and disbursed (September/ October 2009)
short term loan of %50 lakh to the company. The loan*’, secured against
personal guarantee’ of Principal Promoter-Director of the Company, was
repayable in three instalments between November 2009 and May 2010.
Moreover, as provided in the share subscription agreement, Principal Promoter
and the Company’s holding company were to jointly buy back shares held by
WBIDC on completion of three years from the commencement of commercial
production. Instead of enforcing the agreement, WBIDC allowed Principal
Promoter and holding company to defer buy-back of those shares from
contractual date of June 2009 to September 2010/ February 2011. Further,
instead of buy-back of those shares by Principal Promoter and holding
company, WBIDC now sought buy-back by the company. This was contrary
to provision of the agreement (June 2005) which amounted to undue favour to
Principal Promoter and holding company.

While evaluating the proposal, WBIDC obtained company’s VAT registration
certificate along with other registration certificates. WBIDC did not verify the
validity of the registration certificates obtained. Audit observed that its VAT
registration was cancelled in April 2009. Yet, WBIDC not only sanctioned
(August 2009) loan but had extended the buyback period without proper
checks. In June and September 2010, the banks took over" the assets of the
private sector company.

Thus, due to defective project appraisal as well as disbursement of loan
against flawed security, WBIDC sustained loss of = 95 lakh.

+# Where the agency (WBTIDCL) provides the bus and the permit belongs to the private
operator.

for own operation whereas 60 air-conditioned ultra low floor (AULF) buses, 49 air-
